This Report for the year ended 31 March 2014 has been prepared for
submission to the Governor of the State of Punjab under Article 151 of
the Constitution of India.

The Report contains significant results of the performance audit and
compliance audit of the departments/autonomous bodies of Government
of Punjab under the Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-Public
Sector Undertakings).

The instances mentioned in this Report are those, which came to notice
in the course of test audit during the period 2013-14 as well as those
which came to notice in earlier years, but could not be reported in the
previous Audit Reports; instances relating to the period subsequent to
2013-14 have also been included, wherever necessary.

The audit has been conducted in conformity with the Auditing Standards
issued by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India.



Overview



Overview

This Report contains four performance audits on (i) Rashtriya Krishi Vikas
‘Yojana; (ii) Working of Printing and Stationary Department; (iii) Management
and disposal of bio-medical waste; and (iv)Working of Social Security and
‘Women and Child Development Department; and 22 paragraphs involving
money value of ~ 294.26 crore. Some of the major findings are mentioned
below:

The total expenditure of the State increased by 48.66 per cent from
% 29,603 crore to रैं 44,007 crore, the revenue expenditure increased by
51.93 per cent from % 27,408 crore to रैं 41,641 crore, while the capital
expenditure increased by 1.62 per cent from हैं 2,166 crore to % 2,201 crore
during the period 2009-14.

A performance audit of ‘Working of Printing and Stationery Department’
was conducted. Some major findings are mentioned below:

>

Despite provision of funds, the much-needed plan of modernization of
printing presses could not be implemented.

(Paragraph 2.2.6.1)

Receipts on account of cost of printing, supply of various forms, etc.
amounting to ~ 13.30 crore as of November 2014 were yet to be
realized from commercial departments.

(Paragraph 2.2.7.2(i))

Despite budget provisions, bills were not passed by treasury to clear
pending liabilities of ¥ 2.82 crore towards cost of work done from UT
Press, Chandigarh.

(Paragraph 2.2.7.3)

Printing machines remained underutilized to the extent of 61 per cent
as per norms fixed by the Department and 44 per cent of printing
orders were outsourced by the Department during 2009-14. Out of 150
pending printing jobs as of March 2014 with Government Presses,
SAS Nagar and Patiala, 112 jobs pertained to the calendar years 2003
to 2013.

(Paragraphs 2.2.8.1 & 2.2.8.2)

Overhead charges were not revised since 2004-05 leading to unrealistic
job costing. Various old publications, printed forms and registers;
unserviceable machines; and damaged paper having book value of
4.11 crore were awaiting disposal.

(Paragraphs 2.2.8.3, 2.2.9.2 & 2.2.9.3)

A performance audit of ‘Management and disposal of bio-medical waste’
was conducted. Some major findings are mentioned below:

>

Punjab Pollution Control Board (PPCB) did not have any
comprehensive list of Health Care Establishments (HCEs) functioning
in the State. PPCB had identified only 245 Government HCEs
(eight per cent) against 3176 Government HCEs functional in the
State. Sixty per cent of the identified HCEs were functioning without
valid authorisation.

(Paragraphs 2.3.6.1 & 2.3.6.2)

Eighty per cent of test-checked Government HCEs did not have proper
arrangement for disinfection of liquid waste through chemical
treatment and the untreated liquid waste was being discharged in the
drains causing pollution to surrounding water bodies.

(Paragraph 2.3.8.4 (iii))

Compliance Audit

Vv

Vv

The Department purchased a helicopter valuing हैं 36.62 crore in
disregard of the Rules prescribed for public procurement.

(Paragraph 3.1)

Disabled students were deprived of the benefits of the scheme due to
diversion, non-utilization and non-release of Central assistance of
% 17.38 crore earmarked for them.

(Paragraph 3.3)

The Department did not identify food business operators without
licenses/registration in the State. Shortfall in collection of samples
remained up to 80 per cent. Analysis of samples delayed up to
14 months. Prosecution against the offenders had not been launched
even after 31 months.

(Paragraph 3.5)

Arms valuing % 2.17 crore procured without compatible ammunition;
without ensuring requisite specifications; and without assessing
requirement led to idle expenditure. Central Sales Tax of 3.35 crore
was paid in excess of the prescribed rates. Custody of arms and
ammunition was not as per norms and those were not being
periodically examined for its serviceability due to non-availability of
Ammunition Examiner.

(Paragraph 3.7)

Tnaction on the part of Punjab Urban Development Authority (PUDA)
to safeguard recovery of loan before rescinding the existing escrow
agreement executed in its favour, led to non-recovery of loan of
& 221.64 crore and interest of ¥ 28.52 crore.

(Paragraph 3.8)

Failure of Chief Town Planner to raise claim on PUDA and concerned
Development Authorities on account of planning charges resulted in
non-recovery of € 59.50 crore.

(Paragraph 3.9)

Non-release of funds by the State Government despite availability of
Central funds and funds from Government of Rajasthan resulted in
blockade of funds of = 123.09 crore and denial of intended benefits to
the farmers.

(Paragraph 3.10)

Non-providing of clear site by the Department led to delay in
completion of the work which resulted in avoidable payment of
र₹ 9.73 crore on account of cost escalation.

Overview

Action of Chief Engineer to amend the pre-qualification criteria to the

Failure of the Executive Engineers to deduct Value Added Tax at the

Cattle fair ground, Nabipur modernized at a cost of € 7.30 crore could
not be used for holding cattle fairs even after three years due to non-

(Paragraph 3.18)

Deficient planning led to irregular/excess expenditure of ¥ 14.84 crore
on installation of Reverse Osmosis Plants. Viability Gap Funding

र₹ 1.62 crore were found non-functional.

(Paragraph 3.20)

Annual Implementation Plan was prepared without consolidation of
Gram Panchayat/Block/District Plans. Non-utilization of funds of
% 13.57 crore, short achievement of targets and non-availability of

Financial assistance, under Shagun scheme, was provided to 106393

1.2 Application of resources of the State Government

During 2013-14, against the total budget outlay of % 1,00,719 crore,
expenditure of % 95,314 crore was incurred. The total expenditure! of the
State increased by 48.66 per cent from %29,603 crore in 2009-10 to
% 44,007 crore in 2013-14, Revenue expenditure increased by 51.93 per cent
from हैं 27,408 crore to % 41,641 crore; non-plan revenue expenditure
increased by 48.19 per cent from X 25,984 crore to हैं 38,506 crore; and capital
expenditure increased by 1.62 per cent from % 2,166 crore to हैं 2,201 crore
during the period 2009-14.

Revenue expenditure constituted a dominant proportion (93 to 95 per cent) of
the total expenditure during the years 2009-14 leaving small fund for capital
expenditure (5 to 7 per cent). During this period, it grew at an annual average
growth rate of 10.39 per cent, whereas revenue receipts grew at an annual
average of 11.69 per cent during 2009-14.

' Total expenditure includes revenue expenditure, capital expenditure and loans and

advances.

1.4 Funds transferred to State implementing agencies outside State
budget

During 2013-14, Government of India (GOI) directly transferred
1751.30 crore to various State implementing agencies (Para 1.2.3 of
Comptroller and Auditor General’s Audit Report on State Finances for the
year 2013-14 refers). Since these funds do not pass through the State budget,
the Annual Finance Accounts of the State do not present a complete picture of
fund flow of Central Government resources into the State.

1.6. Planning and conduct of audit

The Audit process starts with a risk assessment of various departments,
autonomous bodies, schemes/projects etc. based on expenditure incurred,
criticality/complexity of activities, level of delegated financial powers, internal
controls and concerns of stake holders. Previous audit findings are also
considered in this exercise. Based on this risk assessment, the frequency and
extent of audit are decided and an Annual Audit Plan is formulated to conduct
audit, covering those offices/entities which are vulnerable to significant risks.

After completion of audit of each office, an Inspection Report containing audit
findings is issued to the head of the office with request to furnish replies to the
audit findings within four weeks of its receipt. The important audit



Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

observations are processed for inclusion in the Audit Reports of the
Comptroller and Auditor General of India, which are submitted to the
Governor of Punjab under Article 151 of the Constitution of India.

During 2013-14, compliance audit of 1562 drawing and disbursing officers of
the State, 419 PRIs/ULBs and 15 autonomous bodies was conducted by the
office of the Principal Accountant General (Audit), Punjab. Besides, four
performance audits” were also conducted.

1.7. Significant audit observations and response of Government to
audit

In the last few years, Audit has reported on several significant deficiencies in
implementation of various programmes/activities as well as on the quality of
internal controls in selected departments, which have impact on the success of
programmes and functioning of the departments. The focus was on auditing
the specific programmes/schemes and to offer suitable recommendations to
the executive for taking corrective action and improving service delivery to
the citizens.

As per instructions issued by the Finance Department, Government of Punjab
in August 1992 and provision of Comptroller and Auditor General of India's
Regulations on Audit and Accounts, 2007, the departments are required to
send their response to draft performance audit reports/draft paragraphs
proposed for inclusion in the Comptroller and Auditor General of India's Audit
Reports within six weeks of their receipt. It was brought to their personal
attention that in view of likely inclusion of such paragraphs in the Reports of
the Comptroller and Auditor General of India, to be placed before the Punjab
Legislature, it would be desirable to include their comments in the matter.
They were also advised to have meetings with the Principal Accountant
General to discuss the draft reports of performance audits and draft audit
paragraphs. The performance audits and the draft paragraphs proposed for
inclusion in this Report were also forwarded to the Principal
Secretaries/Secretaries concerned for seeking their replies. For the present
Audit Report, draft reports on four performance audits and 22 draft paragraphs
on compliance audit were forwarded to the concerned Administrative
Secretaries. Reply of the Government has been received in five* cases only.

1.8 Recoveries at the instance of audit

The audit findings involving recoveries that came to notice during audit were
referred to the departments/State Government through Inspection Reports for
further investigation and recovery of the same under intimation to Audit. On
being pointed out in Audit, हैं 4.47 crore were recovered during 2013-14.

(i) Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana; (ii) Working of Printing and Stationary Department;
(iii) Management and disposal of bio-medical waste; and (iv) Working of Social Security
and Women and Child Development Department.

(i) Purchase of helicopter in a non-transparent manner; (ii) Avoidable burden on State
exchequer; (iii) Inadmissible expenditure from State Disaster Response Fund;
(iv) Unfruitful expenditure on modernization of cattle fair ground; and (v) Non-
percolation of intended benefits to rural BPL youth.



Chapter-1: Introduction

1.9 Lack of responsiveness of Government to Audit

The Principal Accountant General (Audit), Punjab conducts periodical
inspection of Government Departments by test-check of transactions and
verifies the maintenance of important accounting and other records as per the
prescribed rules and procedures. These inspections are followed by issue of
Inspection Reports (IR) which are required to be replied to within four weeks
of their receipt. Serious irregularities are also brought to the notice of the
heads of the departments by the office of the Principal Accountant General
(Audit), Punjab through a half yearly report of pending IRs sent to the
Principal Secretary (Finance).

As of March 2014, 20229 IRs containing 40975 paragraphs were outstanding,
of which, 13221 IRs containing 17953 paragraphs pertained to the period prior
to April 2009 i.e. more than five years old. The year-wise position of
outstanding IRs/paragraphs is given in Table 1.4.

Inspection 13221 1175 1232 1342 1583 1676 | 20229
Reports
Paragraphs 17953 2489 | 2800 | 4051 5674 | 8008 | 40975 |

Pendency of large number of paragraphs indicated lack of responsiveness of
the Government departments to Audit.

1.10 Follow-up action on Audit Reports

At the instance of the Public Accounts Committee (PAC), the Finance
Department issued (August 1992) instructions to all the Departments to initiate
suo moto concrete action on all paragraphs and reviews featuring in the Audit
Reports irrespective of whether the cases were taken up for examination by
PAC or not. The Departments were also required to furnish to PAC detailed
notes, duly vetted by Audit, indicating the remedial action taken or proposed
to be taken by them within a period of three months of the presentation of the
Reports to the State Legislature.

As regards the Audit Reports relating to the period 2009-13 which have been
laid before the State Legislature, detailed notes in respect of 22 paragraphs and
six performance audits had not been received in the Audit office as on

three months.

AGRICULTURE, DAIRY DEVELOPMENT & FISHERIES AND

21 Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana

Government of India launched (August 2007) a Special Additional Central
Assistance Scheme namely Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana (RKVY) with a view
to achieve four per cent annual growth in the agriculture and allied sectors
by ensuring holistic development of agriculture and allied sectors and to
maximize returns to farmers during XI" five year plan period.

Some of the significant findings of the Performance Audit of RKVY are given

below:

> ₹ 21.31 crore was incurred on areas/activities which overlapped with
other existing schemes of Government of India/State and % 1.17 crore
was utilized for works not related to RKVY.

(Paragraphs 2.1.6.1 & 2.1.8.1)

> Loss of interest of € 4.12 crore due to retention of RKVY funds in
current account was noticed. Interest of % 4.42 crore earned on RKVY

funds was not accounted for towards RKVY.
(Paragraph 2.1.7.2)

of

> Instances of blockade of funds (₹ 7 crore); irregular award 9;
work/undue favour to NGOs (* 15.16 crore); excess/irregular payment of
subsidy (2.02 crore); and unfruitful/wasteful expenditure (€ 5.20 crore)
were noticed.

(Paragraphs 2.1.8.2, 2.1.9.1(i), 2.1.9.1(ii), 2.1.9.2,
2.1.12.4(i), 2.1.12.4(ii), 2.1.12.5; 2.1.10.2 & 21.111)

targeted annual growth rate of four per cent was not achieved in
agriculture and allied sectors despite increased investment.

(Paragraphs 2.1.13 & 2.1.14)

2.1.1 Introduction

The Ministry of Agriculture, Department of Agriculture and Cooperation,
Government of India (GOT), concerned by the slow growth in the Agriculture
and allied sectors and keeping in view high potential for growth in agriculture


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

sector, launched (August 2007) a Special Additional Central Assistance
Scheme namely Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana (RKVY) in all States and
Union Territories from the year 2007-08 with the aim of achieving
four per cent annual growth in the agriculture sector during the XI" Five Year
Plan period 2007-12 by ensuring a holistic development of agriculture and
allied sectors. The areas of focus under RKVY were integrated development
of major crops, agriculture mechanization, soil health, support to seed farms,
pest management, horticulture production and popularization of micro
irrigation systems, animal husbandry and fisheries development, organic and
bio-fertilizers, crop diversification etc.

2.1.2 Organizational set up

At State level, Agriculture Department, Punjab is the Nodal Department for
implementation of RKVY. A State Level Sanctioning Committee (SLSC)
constituted (December 2007) under the Chairmanship of Chief Secretary to
Government of Punjab (GOP) sanctions the projects at State level and reviews
the implementation of the scheme. The Nodal Department ensures that the
central assistance released under the scheme is utilized in accordance with the
approved State and District Agriculture Plans. Directors, Agriculture, Animal
Husbandry, Dairy Development, Horticulture, Fisheries Departments and
Chief Conservator of Soils, Punjab implemented various projects under
RKVY falling under their respective jurisdiction.

2.1.3 Audit objectives
The main objectives of Performance Audit of RKVY were to assess whether:

> Planning process of RKVY conformed to its guidelines;

Vv

Financial management ensured adequate and timely availability of funds
and their effective and economic utilisation;

> Implementation of the Scheme was carried out economically, efficiently
and effectively to achieve its objectives; and

> Internal control mechanism was in place.
2.1.4 Scope of audit and methodology

For Performance Audit of RKVY six' (out of total 15) sectors were selected
by adopting probability proportional to size without replacement (PPSWOR)
method of statistical sampling. Further, by using PPSWOR, 21 (out of 75
projects within the six selected sectors and 19 districts (Appendix-2.1)
(covering five districts for each project, common districts being selected first)
were selected for test checking the implementation of the scheme. Besides
this, Crop Diversification Programme was also selected for performance audit.
Audit scrutinized the records relating to implementation of RKVY for the

1.0) Agriculture Research; (ii) Animal Husbandry; (iii) Micro and Minor Irrigation

(iv) Dairy Development; (v) Fertilizers and Integrated Nutrients Management ; and
(vi) Seed.



Chapter-2: Performance Audit

period 2009-14 maintained by the offices of the Director, Agriculture, Punjab?
and other executing agencies’ and their subordinate offices that implemented
the selected projects, during April to August 2014. Audit teams along with the
Departments’ representatives also carried out joint inspection of the selected
project sites in selected districts for physical verification of beneficiaries.

An Entry Conference with the representatives of Department of Agriculture,
Punjab and Line Departments was held on 25 April 2014 wherein the audit
objectives, criteria and methodology were explained. Audit findings were
discussed in the Exit Conference held on 25 November 2014 in which the
Commissioner, Agriculture assured to furnish detailed replies within a week.
Thereafter, the Director of Agriculture, the Nodal Department, furnished
(December 2014) replies to the audit observations which have suitably been
incorporated in the report.

2.1.5 Audit criteria

The audit findings were bench marked against the criteria derived from the
following sources:

> Guidelines of RKVY issued by GOI;

> Agenda and Minutes of the meetings of State Level Sanctioning
Committee;

> Instructions/guidelines issued from time to time by GOI/State
Government for implementation of the Scheme and _ various
projects; and

> Punjab Financial Rules.

2.1.6 Planning
2.1.6.1 Deficiencies in planning
Scrutiny of records of the Nodal Department showed that:

> State Level Sanction Committee met only nine times during 2009-14
against the requirement of 20 meetings. The Director, Agriculture while
admitting the facts stated (December 2014) that in 2015-16, the projects would
be sanctioned during March 2015 and sufficient time would be available for
convening at least three SLSC meetings annually.

> No sector wise milestones to achieve the targeted annual growth rate of
four per cent were laid down. The Director, Agriculture stated that the yearly
targets for achieving targeted growth rate were fixed while preparing the
Annual Plan. The reply did not explain the reasons for not fixing the sector
wise benchmarks or milestones for achieving the targeted growth rate of
four per cent.

? Nodal Department for RKVY as well as executing agency for two selected sectors viz.

Fertilizers and Integrated Nutrients Management and Seed.
The Directors, Animal Husbandry, Dairy Development and Fisheries, Chief Conservator
of Soils, Punjab, Principal Chief Conservator of Forests, Punjab, Punjab Agricultural
University Ludhiana and Guru Angad Dev Veterinary and Animal Sciences University,
Ludhiana; and Secretary Punjab Mandi Board.

3

9


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

> Till 2009-10, the Nodal Department recommended projects for
approval of SLSC without Detailed Project Reports in violation of paras 7.1.1
and 7.1.2 of RKVY guidelines. In the absence of DPRs, the spirit of RKVY to
give it a project approach was defeated which in turn deprived the State of
benefits of adoption of project approach.

The Director, Agriculture stated (December 2014) that the proposals
submitted to the SLSC were considered as Project Reports and after directions
of GOI (March 2010) to the States that while preparing project proposals, a
Data Sheet/DPR of the project should also be prepared, the data sheet/DPR
was attached with Agenda Notes for the SLSC meeting since 2010-11. The
reply was not acceptable as DPRs were to be prepared ever since the
implementation of RKVY and approval of SLSC was also to be obtained.

> Para 6.3(4) of the Guidelines of RKVY provides that SLSC would
ensure that no duplication of efforts of resources takes place. During audit of
the Nodal Department, instances involving हैं 21.31 crore‘ from RKVY relating
to overlapping of activities/areas with other schemes of State/Centre were
noticed,

The Director, Agriculture stated (December 2014) that coverage of additional
area or provision of additional equipment by sourcing funds from other
sources was not duplication but it ensured a greater coverage. The reply was
not convincing as overlapping of RKVY activities/areas with other schemes of
State/Centre was in violation of guidelines of RKVY as well as Macro
Management Work Plan of Agriculture, one of the central schemes with
which the activities were overlapped.

Chapter-2: Performance Audit

Analysis of the above table showed that expenditure during 2009-10 and
2010-11 fell short by 38 and 40 per cent respectively of the total funds
available. During 2011-12 and 2012-13, GOP short released the GOI funds by
% 76.43 crore and हैं 50.10 crore which were released during 2012-13 and
2013-14 respectively. In 2013-14, the Director, Agriculture did not draw
% 27 crore released by GOI and GOP. The closing balance of हैं 29.98 crore
includes हैं 26.68 crore retained by the Director, Agriculture out of the funds
drawn (% 112.25 crore®) for Crop Diversification Programme (CDP). This
resulted in non-implementation of the projects approved by SLSC for which
the funds were received from GOI.

Audit further noticed that GOP released % 657.49 crore during 2009-14 to
Nodal Department with a delay ranging between 65 and 255 days. Further,
11 to 37 per cent funds were released during last quarter of the respective
financial years. Similarly, delay in release of funds of ¥ 635.10 crore by Nodal
Department to the implementing agencies ranged between two and 180 days.
Delay in release of funds was in violation of the Para 7.2.4 of RKVY
guidelines which provides for utilization of funds by the State and GOI’s
instructions (March 2012) to ensure that funds are released to the Nodal
Department by the Finance Department (FD) as soon as they are received from
GOI. Thus, implementing agencies were left with no or little time to execute
the projects resulting in spilling over of execution of the projects to the next
year.

The Director, Agriculture while admitting the above facts stated
(December 2014) that sometimes there was a delay in release of funds by the
State due to financial constraints and relative priority for release to various
economic sectors which were further released to the implementing agencies
keeping in view the immediate requirements. The reply was not acceptable as
the funds were received from GOI against the projects approved by SLSC and
the plea of financial constraints was not convincing as 100 per cent funds
under RKVY were received from GOI.

2.1.7.2 | Non-accountal of interest

As per Instructions of GOI of April 2010, interest earned on GOI funds was to
be counted towards Grant-in-Aid for respective scheme funds. During audit,
the following deficiencies were noticed:

6) During April 2010 to July 2013, the Director, Agriculture kept RKVY
funds ranging between % 0.01 crore and हैं 69.43 crore in a current account
which led to loss of % 1.36 crore on account of interest (calculated at interest
rate on savings bank account). The Director, Agriculture stated
(December 2014) that interest rates in different banks were different, so a
current account was opened in State Bank of India. The reply was not
acceptable as the Department should have opened savings account in a bank
with higher rate of interest instead of opening current account on which no
interest at all was earned.

5 ₹ 85.57 crore were disbursed to the implementing agencies.

11


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

(ii) | Managing Director, MILKFED (MD) kept entire RKVY funds in two
Over Draft (OD) Accounts’ on which MILKFED saved interest of = 2.76 crore
during the period from 31 December 2009 to 31 March 2014 on ODs availed
by it without affording credit to RKVY. The Director, Agriculture stated
(December 2014) that the credit of the interest earned would be given to
RKVY funds.

(iii) During July 2009-July 2014, interest of € 4.42 crore’ earned on RKVY
funds was not counted towards grant-in-aid for RKVY by reflecting the same
in UCs. The Director, Agriculture stated (December 2014) that the Nodal
Department had issued (November 2014) instructions to all the implementing
agencies for accounting the interest earned towards the future funds as an
additional grant-in-aid under RKVY.

2.1.7.3 Withdrawal of money without immediate requirement

Rule 2.10 (b) (5) of PFR provides that no money should be withdrawn from
the treasury unless it is required for immediate disbursement.

Scrutiny of records of the Director, Agriculture Punjab showed (July 2013)
that during March 2008 to November 2010, ₹ 19.83 crore® withdrawn from
treasury were disbursed to the implementing agencies with delay ranging
between 67 and 231 days.

The Director, Agriculture stated (December 2014) that the funds released by
GOI were further released by the State to the Nodal Agency as per priority of
the State which were then released for implementing the projects as per actual
physical requirements. The reply was not acceptable as the money was
received against the already approved projects. Thus, late disbursement to the
implementing agencies was not justified and in case the funds were not to be
disbursed immediately then these should have not been withdrawn from the
treasury.

21.7.4 Irregularities in submission of utilization certificate

Audit of records of Nodal Department showed that UCs were not sent to GOI
in the prescribed format and important information relating to targets achieved
with the funds utilized was not given as required under Para 2 of the
prescribed format. Audit further noticed that in 15 cases involving
% 44.14 crore, the implementing agencies’ submitted UCs to the Nodal

2.1.7.5 | Non-utilisation of RKVY funds

Funds of RKVY were to be utilized in the year of its sanction. However,
% 6.74 crore sanctioned for various projects (as detailed in Appendix-2.2)
during 2009-10 and 2012-14 were not utilized by the implementing agencies
till July 2014. The Director, Agriculture stated (December 2014) that now all
the funds released under RKVY up to 2012-13 had been utilized and UCs
submitted to GOI. The reply was not acceptable as the fact remained that the
funds were not utilized during the year of sanction itself.

For test checking the implementation of programmes under RKVY in the
State, 21 projects under six Sectors were selected (as already discussed in
paragraph 2.1.4). The significant findings noticed during the Performance
Audit are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs:

2.1.8 Agriculture Research (AGRE)

During 2009-14, eleven projects with approved cost of € 106.21 crore were

% 51.09 crore) under AGRE sector. The Nodal Department released % 51 crore

(one project: हैं 15 crore) at a cost of € 24 crore were selected for test check,
the findings of which are given below:

2.1.8.1 Utilization of funds for works not related to RKVY

Out of % 15 crore received (August-December 2010) by GADVASU for a
research project'? under RKVY for the year 2010-11, the Comptroller,
GADVASU utilized (31 March 2011) ₹ 1.17 crore for installation of a Hotline
electricity connection (% one crore) and for purchase of server, chassis and
server rack for the University Library (रे 17.20 lakh'') not covered under the

Enhancing Live Stock Production in Punjab through need based Research and

These items were already sanctioned under “University Administrative Scheme NP-I”

project. The Hotline was installed in December 2013. The funds were drawn
from RKVY on the pretext of non-availability of funds from any other source.

The Director, Agriculture while justifying the expenditure stated that revised

under RKVY.

2.1.8.2 Blockade of funds

The Comptroller, GADVASU, after ex-post-facto approval (June 2011) of
SLSC, allocated (July 2011) € seven crore for setting up an Animal Hospital
at GADVASU. Audit noticed that funds were allocated for the work even
before its administrative approval (20 August 2013). The work was allotted
to a contractor only in May 2014. Thus, the funds remained blocked for
36 months from July 2011 to July 2014 which could have otherwise been
utilized for other productive purposes.

The Director, Agriculture attributed (December 2014) the delay in execution
of Hospital to late allocation of land by Punjab Agricultural University. The
reply was not acceptable as the availability of land should have been ensured
before allocation of funds and RKVY funds of हैं seven crore had remained
blocked for 36 months.

2.1.9 Dairy Development

During 2009-14, 27 projects with approved cost of हैं 126.95 crore were
implemented by Director, Dairy Development under the sector. Nodal
Department released % 64.12 crore against which the Department incurred an
expenditure of € 57.18 crore on implementation of these projects. Six projects
implemented by the Director, Dairy Development at a cost of % 24.68 crore
were selected for test check, the findings of which are given below:

2.1.9.1 Integrated Buffalo Development Centres’ project

With a view to provide services of artificial insemination (AI) in case of
Buffaloes and related services through establishment of 600 Integrated Buffalo
Development Centres (IBDCS) in Punjab under the project ‘Establishment of
Integrated Buffalo Development Centres’, MILKFED"” invited
(September 2009) tenders from NGOs/Private Registered Organizations
working in the field for at least five years. Audit noticed the following points:

fi) Allotment of work to a non-bidder

Audit of records of MILKFED disclosed that only one NGO viz. JK Trust
submitted bid for establishment of 300 IBDCs at हैं 14.83 lakh per IBDC,
whereas another NGO viz. BAIF" Development Research Foundation (8417)
had neither submitted requisite processing fee nor technical and financial bids

Chapter-2: Performance Audit

but gave only a proposal and hence did not enter into the bid. The Tender
Committee recommended (December 2009) that JK Trust be allotted the work
of establishment of 300 IBDCs. During a meeting held on 11 February 2010
under the Chairmanship of Chief Minister, Punjab, it was decided to place
orders on JK Trust for establishing 200 IBDCs at the rate of
र₹ 14.83 lakh per IBDC and BAIF for 100 IBDCs at the rates of JK Trust (as
BAIF had not quoted its rates). However, MILKFED placed (March 2010) the

was exempted from payment of bank guarantee of % 88 lakh required under
terms and conditions of NIT. Thus, allotment of work of establishing 100
IBDCs to BAIF which even did not submit its bid, was an undue financial
benefit to the NGO which has been paid % 13.40 crore till July 2014. This
along with change in number of IBDCs for which tenders were invited,
allotment of work only for 100 IBDCs to JK Trust vitiated the tendering
process in allotment of the said work.

The Director, Agriculture stated (December 2014) that considering the good
track record of BAIF, the decision to allot a part of work to it and exempt from
furnishing bank guarantee was taken in a meeting held on 11 February 2010
under the Chairmanship of Chief Minister, Punjab. Change in number of
IBDCs, for which tenders were invited and the work was actually allotted, was
attributed to the shortage of funds The reply was not acceptable as allotment
of work to BAIF which did not even participate in the tendering process, as
per the conditions of the tender notice, vitiated the tendering process as once
the tenders were invited, only the successful bidder complying with all the laid
down requirements should have been allotted the work.

(ii) Irregular release of preparatory expenses

Audit noticed that the Managing Director, MILKFED (MD), instead of
making 50 per cent payment ( 0.88 crore) towards preparatory expenses as
per the agreement with JK Trust and BAIF, paid (April-May 2010) 100
per cent amount of € 1.76 crore to both the NGOs.

The Director, Agriculture stated (December 2014) that preparatory expenses
were released in one installment as per decision taken by Project
Implementation and Monitoring Committee (PIMC) in its meeting held on
30 October 2009. The reply was not acceptable because as per agreements,
only 50 per cent advance payment towards preparatory expenses was to be
made and the balance after proof of expenses incurred on infrastructure was
provided.

(iii) _ Irregular retention of AI charges out of Government account

(a) During October 2010 to March 2014, NGOs collected % 2.30 crore
from the beneficiaries on account of artificial insemination (AI) charges" in
respect of 768153 AI. Instead of depositing the same in Government account,
the amount was irregularly retained in a Corpus Fund of MILKFED. The
Director, Agriculture stated (December 2014) that PIMC in its meeting held

(b) — The Managing Director, MILKFED enhanced AI charges from हैं 3010

(vy) Non-achievement of targeted production of Buffalo calves

calves born up to October 2014 was more than that of the targeted number.
This project would complete in the month of December 2015 and as per the
pregnancy confirmation report of BAIF, 140096 and 104783 buffalo and cow
calves respectively would be born. Similarly, JK Trust reported that 136364
and 80213 buffalo and cow calves would be born respectively. The reply was
not acceptable as even after considering the data up to October 2014, the
percentage of buffalo calves produced by BAIF and JK was 58 and 64
respectively against the target of 80 per cent. Even after taking into
consideration the projected data based on pregnancy confirmation report of

both the NGOs, the percentage of buffalo calves produced by BAIF and JK

(vi) Non-monitoring of the project

A state level monitoring cell at MILKFED, Head Office as well as at Milk
Plants was to be established to monitor the whole IBDC Project However, test
check of the records of MILKFED showed that monitoring of the project was
not being done. In the absence of this, the actual outcome of the project could
not be ascertained in audit.

The Director, Agriculture stated (December 2014) that the project was being
closely monitored by his office, the Financial Commissioner Development-
cum-Vice Chairman of SLSC and Managing Director, Milkfed. The reply was
silent on monitoring of the progress by the State and Milk Plant Level
Monitoring Cells and the progress reports, annexed to the reply were prepared
by the NGOs, just depicted the number of calves born for claiming payment.

2.1.9.2 Irregular disbursement of subsidy

As per guidelines issued (May 2011) by the Director Dairy Development
Punjab (Director) for implementation of ‘Special Programme on Dairy
Development’, subsidy was to be given at the rate of 25 per cent of cost of
milching animals subject to a maximum of € 12500 per animal for purchase of
15 to 25 animals to beneficiaries who did not have more than five milching
animals. Scrutiny of the records of the Director showed that in seven cases,
subsidy of हैं 18.75 lakh was disbursed (January to March 2012) to the
beneficiaries having more than five milching animals.

The Director, Agriculture stated (December 2014) that in the guidelines
governing Special Programme for Dairy Development under National Mission
on Protein Supplement, there was no condition stipulating the number of
animals with the beneficiary. The reply was not acceptable as the guidelines

stipulated the condition ibid.

2.1.10 Micro and minor irrigation

During 2010-14, five projects having approved cost of हैं 11] crore were
implemented by the Chief Conservator of Soils, Punjab under the sector.
Nodal Department released % 81.27 crore against which (CCS), Punjab
incurred expenditure of € 70.52 crore. One project ‘Conservation of Irrigation
Water Project’ implemented at a cost of € 49 crore during 2010-11 was test
checked, findings of which are given below:

2.1.10.1 | Imbalanced implementation of the project

In view of the problems like soil degradation and ground water
decline/deterioration, Department of Soil and Water Conservation, Punjab
formulated (2010-11) an action plan for Conservation and Development of
Soil and Water resources for sustainable production in Punjab under RKVY,
laying thrust on a series of measures like conservation of irrigation water,
rainwater harvesting and recharge, reclamation of degraded soils and
watershed development. Accordingly, a Project, ‘Conservation of Irrigation
Water Project? was implemented under RKVY. During 2009-2014,
% 130.86 crore!’ were disbursed to 13 Divisional Soil Conservation Officers
covering 20 districts of the State for execution of the project. Of these,
% 55.34 crore (42.29 per cent) were disbursed to Sri Muktsar Sahib district
only and the farmers of remaining 19 districts got funds ranging between
0.2 per cent and 16.65 per cent only, whereas Jalandhar and Kapurthala
districts were not covered at all under this project which indicated imbalanced
implementation of the project.

The Director, Agriculture stated (December 2014) that major emphasis while
implementing this project was given on ecologically handicapped areas in the
South-Western districts where groundwater is not fit for irrigation and the
saving of seepage and conveyance losses helps in providing better irrigation
facilities in outlet commands. In Jalandhar and Kapurthala, groundwater is fit
for irrigation. The reply was not convincing as non-covering the other areas of
the State which were facing a problem of decline in water table would further
aggravate the situation in these areas and as per the action plan, the project
was to be implemented not only in South-Western districts of Punjab but also
in over exploited blocks of central districts and sub-mountainous areas of
Northern Kandi districts, which got only 5.78 per cent and 4.27 per cent of the
allocations respectively. Jalandhar and Kapurthala, two central districts where
ground water was over exploited'*, were not covered at all.

Chapter-2: Performance Audit

2.1.10.2 Unfruitful expenditure due to non-commissioning of project

The work of providing, laying and jointing of 11880 meter of Under Ground
Pipeline at village Luhara, Doda, tehsil Gidderbaha of district Sri Muktsar
Sahib was allotted (May 2011) for completion up to 17 June 2011. Audit of
records (July 2014) of the Divisional Soil Conservation Officer, Sri Muktsar
Sahib (DSCO) showed that due to change in the alignment of the project, the
length of the pipeline was increased (August 2012) 11880 to 12680 meters
thereby increasing the project cost to हैं 2.69 crore, against which हैं 2.54 crore
were incurred as of March 2013. However, the project could not be made
operational (July 2014) as the pipeline had developed major leakages for more
than 26 times. The Soil Conservation Officer, Gidderbaha (SCO) reported
(July 2014) that the pipeline had some technical fault and was unfit for
operation. Thus, the expenditure of रैं 2.54 crore was wasteful and deprived
the farmers of the intended benefits.

The Director, Agriculture attributed (December 2014) non-commissioning of
project to the dispute between the beneficiary and non-beneficiary farmers and
stated that the laying of pipeline was complete but its successful
commissioning was yet to be accomplished. The reply was not acceptable as
the pipeline could not be commissioned/made operational due to technical
fault as reported by the SCO as well as complaint by the Sarpanch of Luhara
village.

2.1.10.3 Ungainful expenditure on incomplete projects

Audit of records of DSCO, Gurdaspur showed that an expenditure of
& 23.17 lakh and & 18.54 lakh was incurred up to October 2011 and January
2013 on ‘Renovation of Ponds and Retaining walls’ in villages Qila Tek Singh
and Wadala Banger respectively to utilize pond water for agriculture purposes
by installing underground pipeline. The projects were, however, still
(July 2014) incomplete which led to non-percolation of the intended benefits
to the beneficiaries and the entire expenditure of हैं 41.71 lakh remained
ungainful.

In respect of village Qila Tek Singh, the Director, Agriculture stated
(December 2014) that the local farmers requested to include more
beneficiaries. Therefore, a revised estimate was sanctioned in June 2013 and
the work of renovation of pond was complete and laying of pipeline was in
progress (December 2014). The work in village Wadala Banger was started in
2011 and the pipeline was laid from the pond to the farmers’ fields with
available funds. The work of renovation of pond would be completed after
release of balance funds. The reply in respect of village Qila Tek Singh was
not acceptable as the length of pipeline was changed thrice from 500 metres to
2154 metres indicating that estimate of the work initially prepared was not
based on realistic assessment of length of pipeline.

2.1.11.1 | Wasteful expenditure due to abandonment of the project

The Divisional Soil Conservation Officer, Amritsar incurred an expenditure of

at a cost of % 2.66 crore, had gone waste as the farm was full of Kana-
Sarkanda, congress grass, Narri weeds and the whole farm land was in
unlevelled condition. It was also observed (January 2015) that all agricultural
operations were started afresh, beginning with levelling and development of
land into cultivable land. Thus, non-execution of the work as per MOU with
the army rendered the expenditure of % 2.66 crore wasteful as the work of
fencing was dismantled by the army and that of land development was of no
use after remaining abandoned (since February 2012) for two and half years.

2.1.12 Crop diversification programme

To mitigate the problem of depletion of water table and to demonstrate and
promote improved production technologies of alternate crops for diversion
from paddy cultivation and to restore soil fertility, GOI approved (June 2013)
Crop Diversification Programme (CDP) for हैं 224.50 crore (revised to
&% 249.50 crore in February 2014). CDP has four components viz. (i) Cluster
Demonstration; (ii) Farm Mechanisation, processing and value addition,
(iii) Site specific activity; (iv) Contingency for awareness training, implementation
and monitoring. On the basis of quantum of expenditure incurred, five
districts’? were selected for checking the implementation of CDP. Audit
noticed the following irregularities:

Jalandhar, Ludhiana, Mohali, Nawanshahar and Sangrur.

beneficiaries which was not done in all these cases.

whereas in three other districts (Kapurthala, Mohali and Sangrur), the area

to such areas as covered under the guidelines. The reply was not acceptable as
the farmers of Sri Muktsar Sahib district, the worst affected by water logging”
were not covered at all. On the other hand, farmers of Bathinda district having
no water logging were supplied 135000 plants valuing हैं 10.80 lakh.

(iii) Distribution of subsidy

Subsidy of 50 per cent of the cost or हैं 0.50 lakh per hectare, whichever is less,
was to be provided for ‘Improvement in Conveyance Efficiency of Irrigation
Water through Community Underground Pipeline System’(UGPS) given to
the farmers subject to the condition that they would not sow paddy in that area
during the next five years. Though subsidy of % five crore was disbursed
during 2013-14 to the farmers under the project, no system was put in place to
ensure compliance of this vital condition to ensure crop diversification.

The Director, Agriculture stated (December 2014) that the project was
implemented mainly to save conveyance losses on the farmers field and there
was no condition barring the farmers, availing assistance, from growing paddy
in the approved guidelines issued by GOI for implementation of CDP. The
reply was not acceptable as the project prepared by the Department itself
provided that preference would be given to those farmers who gave a written
undertaking not to grow paddy for at least five years after installation of
UGPS on the benefited area owned by them. They would be free to grow any
other alternate crop which was also the main objective of the CDP.

2.1.12.4 Farm Mechanization, processing and value addition

(0) Payment of subsidy in excess of norms

As per action plan approved by GOI, assistance on farm mechanization,
processing and value addition was limited to ¥ 30000 per unit for individual
beneficiary and more than ~ 30000 was only for group of farmers for sharing
or custom hiring of machinery. Similarly, for Multi Crop Thresher, admissible
amount of subsidy was 50 per cent of cost subject to maximum of % 25000 per
unit.

Audit noticed that CAOs, Ludhiana and Mohali disbursed subsidy of
% 35.66 lakh”* to 24 individual farmers at the rate of more than हैं 30000 in
each case against the admissible subsidy of % 7.20 lakh to these farmers. This
resulted into excess payment of % 28.46 lakh. The CAO, Mohali disbursed
subsidy of ~ 30000 per unit (against € 25000 per unit) to two farmers resulting
in excess payment of = 10000.

The Director, Agriculture while admitting the facts stated (December 2014)
that State Government decided that subsidy exceeding ~ 30000 per unit be
allowed to the individual farmers by obtaining an undertaking that the farmer
would provide the machine on custom hiring service to other farmers. As
regards payment of excess subsidy on Multi Crop Thresher, it was stated that
State Level Committee (SLC) enhanced (October 2013) the upper limit to
% 30000. The reply was not acceptable as SLC has to follow GOI norms.

(ii) Inadmissible payment of subsidy

Audit noticed that CAOs, Mohali and Ludhiana disbursed subsidy of
% 27.18 lakh™ to the farmers for purchase of machinery not covered under the
approved action plan. The Director, Agriculture stated (December 2014) that
the agricultural machinery was distributed on subsidy as approved
(August 2013) by SLSC. However, GOI in revised action plan of CDP
disallowed (February 2014) subsidy on this machinery. In the meantime,
some of CAOs had already disbursed subsidy. The fact, however, remained
that the machinery in question was not covered in the original as well as
revised action plan approved by GOI.

Internal control system is a management tool used to provide assurance that
the objectives are being achieved as planned. Audit noticed that:

> The role of SLSC was confined mainly to sanctioning of the projects
and it did not carry out other mandated tasks”. The Director, Agriculture
while admitting the shortfall in meetings, stated (December 2014) that the

Administrative Secretaries of the implementing departments as well as the

(i) Monitoring the progress of the sanctioned projects; (ii) Reviewing the implementation
of the scheme’s objectives and ensure that the programmes are implemented in

resources take place; (iv) Undertaking field studies to monitor the implementation of the
projects etc.

Chapter-2: Performance Audit

Financial Commissioner Development and Director, Agriculture. The fact
remained that SLSC had not exercised its mandate fully.

> The State had not established a Management Information System
(MIS) so far (August 2014) which was required to be established within the
first year of implementation of RKVY. In the Exit Conference, the

> Internal audit of projects under RKVY was not conducted by the Nodal

2.1.14 Non-achievement of prime objective of RKVY

The main aim of RKVY was to achieve four per cent annual growth rate in
agriculture sector during each year of the XI" Five Year Plan (2007-12),
which was not achieved in any of the years and the achievement”’ remained
3.82, 2.03, (-) 0.30, 1.64 and 1.85 per cent (provisional) respectively during
the said period. Thereafter, it declined to 0.16 (quick) and 0.44 per cent
(advance) in 2012-13 and 2013-14 respectively. The Director, Agriculture
stated (December 2014) that increasing the growth rate was a very complex
mechanism depending upon many contributory factors and nature plays an
important role in final output and achievement of four per cent growth rate in
agriculture sector was a long time process. The fact remained that even after
increased investments made under RKVY in the agriculture sector, the
agricultural growth rate in the State had declined from 3.82 per cent in
2007-08 to 0.44 per cent in 2013-14.

Conclusions

The planning process was deficient as no specific benchmarks or milestones
were laid down for achieving the targeted annual growth rate of four per cent.
There was overlapping of area/activities covered under RKVY with the
existing schemes of Central/State Government. Financial management was
deficient as there were instances of short release of central funds by the State
Government to Nodal Department and from the Nodal Department to the
implementing agencies. Cases of loss/non-accountal of interest towards
RKVY funds were noticed. Utilisation certificates were submitted prior to
actual utilization of funds.

Implementation of projects under RKVY was deficient in the instances of
utilization of RK VY funds for works not related to RKVY, blockade of funds;
undue favour to NGOs, irregular payment of subsidy and wasteful expenditure
were noticed. Internal control mechanism was found largely deficient.
Management Information System was not established. Prime objective of
RKVY to achieve four per cent annual growth rate in Agriculture and allied
sectors during each year of the XI" Five Year Plan was not achieved.

Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

Recommendations

The State Government may:

ensure compliance of the provisions of RKVY guidelines to extend
optimum benefits of the scheme to the farmers of the State;

Vv

strengthen financial management system to avoid delay in release of
funds at various levels, ensure proper accountal of interest and correct
submission of utilization certificates; and

Vv

take immediate steps to establish a robust internal control mechanism
including uploading of data at RKVY Database and management
information system.

(February 2015).

Chapter-2: Performance Audit

PRINTING AND STATIONERY DEPARTM.

T

2.2 Working of Printing and Stationery Department

The Printing and Stationery Department is a service department that meets the
requirement of stationery items, paper, forms and registers, etc. of all the
departments of the State Government. The printing jobs are done at the
Government Presses SAS Nagar and Patiala.

Some of the significant findings of the performance audit are given below:

> Despite provision of funds, the much-needed plan of modernization of
printing presses could not be implemented.

(Paragraph 2.2.6.1)

> Receipts on account of cost of printing, supply of various forms, etc.
amounting to € 13.30 crore as of November 2014 were yet to be realized
from commercial departments.

(Paragraph 2.2.7.2(i))

> Despite budget provisions, the bills were not passed by treasury to clear
pending liabilities of €2.82 crore towards cost of work done from UT
Press, Chandigarh.

(Paragraph 2.2.7.3)

> Printing machines remained underutilized to the extent of 61 per cent as
per norms fixed by the Department and 44 per cent of printing orders
were outsourced by the Department during 2009-14. Out of 150 pending
printing jobs as of March 2014 with GPs SAS Nagar and Patiala,
112 jobs pertained to the calendar years 2003 to 2013.

(Paragraphs 2.2.8.1 & 2.2.8.2)

> Overhead charges were not revised since 2004-05 leading to unrealistic
job costing. Various old publications, printed forms and registers;
unserviceable machines; and damaged paper having book value of
4.11 crore were awaiting disposal.

(Paragraphs 2.2.8.3, 2.2.9.2 & 2.2.9.3)

2.2.1 Introduction

The Printing and Stationery Department (Department) was established for the
purpose of executing printing works of Punjab Government; purchase and
supply of stationery; and distribution of forms and Government publications.
The Department executes printing either at Government Presses” (GP) or gets
it outsourced from Union Territory (UT) Press, Chandigarh or from private
presses. The Department is also entrusted with the work of supply and

8 Government presses situated at SAS Nagar and at Patiala.

27


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

maintenance of typewriters, photostat, fax machines, etc. to Government
departments.

2.2.2 Organizational set up

The Secretary is the administrative head and the Controller, Printing and
Stationery (CP&S) is the executive in-charge of the Department. The CP&S
is assisted by Deputy Controller (Technical) for all technical matters and
outsourcing of printing jobs; Deputy Controller, Finance and Accounts
(DCFA) for financial matters at headquarter; and the Assistant Controllers
(AC) are responsible for the day-to-day administration and management of
both the GPs at SAS Nagar and Patiala. Typewriter workshops are headed by
a Superintendent at SAS Nagar, who is assisted by Supervisors at district
level.

2.2.3 Audit Objectives

The main objectives of the performance audit were to assess whether:

> long term plans were formulated and executed;

> financial management was efficient and effective;

v

operations were done by using the available resources optimally;

v

procurement of paper and stationery was consistent with the
requirement and stores were managed efficiently;

> human resource management was effective and efficient; and

Vv

internal control mechanism was in place.

2.2.4 Scope of Audit and methodology

The performance audit on the working of Printing and Stationery Department
was conducted (February 2014-August 2014) by test-checking the records of
the CP&S, GPs at SAS Nagar and Patiala and typewriter workshops, covering
the period 2009-14. Mention was made in the Comptroller and Auditor
General of India's Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 1992 (Paragraph
No. 3.1) - Government of Punjab, on the working of Government Presses.
The findings of report were kept in view while conducting the audit.

An entry conference was held on 19 May 2014 with the Secretary, Printing
and Stationery, wherein the audit objectives, criteria, scope and methodology
were discussed. The audit findings were discussed with the Department in an
exit conference held on 2 December 2014. Replies of the Department have
suitably been incorporated in the report.

2.2.5 Audit criteria

The audit findings were benchmarked against the criteria derived from the
following sources:

Vv

Punjab Printing and Stationery Manual;
Accounting procedure of Printing and Stationery Department, Punjab;

>
>» Schedule of out-turn of the Punjab Government Press; and

Vv

Punjab Financial Rules.

2.2.6 Planning
2.2.6.1 Modernization of Government Presses

In order to upgrade the Government Presses (GP) in the State of Punjab by
providing new machines, additional manpower etc., a State Plan Scheme for
“Modernization of Punjab Government Presses” was formulated during
11" five year plan” (FYP) (2007-12) and 12" FYP* (2012-17) with an outlay
of = 4.27 crore and % 17.41 crore, respectively.

During 117 FYP, the State Government provided funds of 1.55 crore each in
years 2008-09 and 2011-12, respectively, but the same could not be utilized by
the Department. During 12" FYP, as against the release of = 3.97 crore in
2012-14, %1.71 crore were utilized on procurement of new machines.
However, neither the requisite manpower as per the Scheme was deployed nor
was the training to existing manpower planned, leading to under-utilization of
printing machines, as mentioned in paragraph 2.2.8.1.

During exit conference, CP&S assured to take necessary steps for
modernization of Government presses in near future.

2.2.7.2 Unrealized receipts

(0) Receipts from commercial departments

As per Para 83 of the Accounting Procedure, proforma accounts are
maintained to know the value of services rendered to each department and
only in case of few departments which have been declared ‘Commercial’ by
the Government, execution of work is to be done on payment basis and the
charges are realized from them either in cash or by book adjustment. Further,
Para 5.2 of the Printing and Stationery Manual (Manual) provides that the
commercial departments should make payment for the services rendered to
them within 15 days of receipt of the bill.

Audit observed that:

> Proforma accounts were not prepared by the Department in the absence
of which, the value of services rendered to each department could not be
ascertained.

The Department had provided (February 1983) space in the premises of GP

Audit noticed that the rent for the space provided to P&T Department was not
being recovered since February 1983 due to non-assessment of rent and
thereafter from February 2009 despite its assessment by PWD. The
outstanding rent for the period from February 2009 to March 2014 recoverable
from P&T Department worked out to हैं 12.95 lakh*?,

During exit conference, CP&S assured to take up the matter with higher
authorities of P&T Department for effecting recovery of rent as early as
possible.

(iii) Sale proceeds of land

Land measuring 1.34 acre valuing हैं 19.39 crore belonging to the Department,

(June 2009) was transferred (July 2009) to Punjab Urban Development
Authority (PUDA) under Optimum Utilisation of Vacant Government Land
Scheme for its commercial exploitation. As per the Scheme (October 2007),
the sale proceeds realized from the commercial exploitation of the properties
would be credited to Punjab Infrastructure Development Fund™ which would
be prioritized for utilization towards the infrastructure projects pertaining to
the particular department whose properties have been transferred.

Audit observed that the said piece of land transferred to PUDA in July 2009
was not sold out/commercially exploited even after five years
(November 2014), thus, depriving the Department of the privilege to utilize
the sales proceeds of the land for its infrastructure development, especially
when the modernization of Government presses was underway since | 1" FYP.

2.2.7.3 Pending liabilities

Audit of records of CP&S showed that payment of % 2.82 crore for the period
from October 1992 to June 2014 towards the cost of work got done on 980
occasions from UT Press, Chandigarh was yet to be made (November 2014),
thereby creating a liability of ¥ 2.82 crore. During exit conference, CP&S
attributed non-clearance of pending liability to non-passing of bills by the
treasury. The reply was not convincing as the liabilities were accumulating
since long and despite budget provisions, the bills were not passed by treasury
to clear pending liabilities.

2.2.7.4 Delay in deposit of receipts into treasury

Rule 2.4 of PFR Volume-I provides that departmental receipts collected
during the day are credited into the treasury on the same day or on the
morning of the next day at the latest.

Audit of records (May 2014) of CP&S showed that % 36 lakh*® received
during 2009-2014 on account of supply of stationery to commercial
departments were deposited into the treasury with a delay ranging between
two and 38 days due to delay in depositing the money so received by the
Stationery Branch with the Cash Branch. During exit conference, CP&S,
while admitting the omission, stated that strict instructions had been issued to
deposit the receipts into treasury on the same day or the next day.

2.2.8.3 Non-revision of overhead charges

Para 83 of the Accounting Procedure provides that Printing and Stationery

Department being a service Department, would prepare proforma accounts to

2003 (one), 2006(two), 2007 (one), 2010 (two), 2011 (eight), 2012(38) and 2013 (60).

34


Chapter-2: Performance Audit

know the cost of service rendered to each department. The printing cost should
include both direct and indirect expenditure. The direct expenditure relates to
wages of the productive staff, cost of paper, etc., whereas indirect expenditure
(overhead charges) comprise wages of non-productive staff, rent of buildings,
contingent charges, miscellaneous stores, depreciation, interest on plant and
machinery, etc. The overhead charges should be worked out on yearly basis.

Audit of records (May-June 2014) of GPs at SAS Nagar and Patiala showed
that overhead charges were last worked out for the year 2003-04, which were
increased by 25 per cent during 2004-05. Thereafter, these charges had not
been revised. Resultantly, the job costing in respect of non-commercial
departments had not been done by GPs SAS Nagar and Patiala since 2004-05
and 2006-07, respectively. In case of commercial departments, the bills for
the services rendered to them were raised during 2009-14 on the basis of
overhead charges fixed for the year 2004-05. Despite this being pointed out in
the Comptroller and Auditor General's Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended
31 March 1992 (paragraph 3.1.7), job costing was not being done by the
Department due to non-revision of overhead charges. This was not only the
violation of the Accounting Procedure but also led to realization of lesser cost
of printing from commercial departments.

During exit conference, it was stated that with a view to compete with the
market rates, a proposal to revise the calculation pattern for working out
overhead charges was being sent to the Finance Department.

2.2.9 Procurement and stock management

2.2.9.1 Procurement of stationery and paper

(6) The Manual (Paragraph 10.2) requires each Head of the Department to
submit an estimate of stationery during the ensuing financial year together
with schedule of new expenditure and explanatory memorandum. Rule 2.10
of PFR also demands high standards of financial propriety and provides that
expenditure should not be prima facie more than the demand of the occasion.

Audit of records (March 2014) showed that neither the Department called for
nor the indenting departments submitted the estimates for stationery items
during 2009-12. Stationery items were purchased on the basis of issuance of
stationery in previous three years. Resultantly, closing stock of stationery
valuing % 0.22 crore at the end of 2009-10 escalated to हें 1.66 crore at the end
of 2011-12. On the other hand, number of items ranging between 43 and 59
remained out of stock for a period ranging between one month to 36 months
and CP&S issued 86 NOCs to the departments for purchase of these stationery
items from open market during the same period (2009-12). Thereafter, from
2012-13 onwards, the Department complying with the rules, started procuring
stationery by calling estimates from the departments. As a result of this, the
stock was reduced to हैं 0.45 crore at the end of 2012-13".

During exit conference, CP&S stated that the position of inventory was
improving with the calling of demands from the field offices.

“Store account in respect of Stationery for 2013-14 was not prepared (November 2014).

Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

(ii) Rule 15.2(b) of PFR provides that purchases must be made in most
economical manner and in accordance with the definite requirement of the

Audit of records of GP SAS Nagar showed that the Department procured

2.2.9.2 Non-disposal of old publications and printing jobs

@ The Department has a Book Depot wherein all priced publications of
Government are stocked for sale. As per Manual, the department concerned
under whose authority a publication was published should review, from time
to time, the disposal of unsold copies left in stock.

Audit of records (June 2014) of the Book Depot at GP SAS Nagar showed that
265026 copies of various publications having selling price of हैं 94 lakh were
lying in the store for more than five years and were not reviewed by the
departments concerned for their disposal.

(ii) Similarly, Audit also noticed that 19.55 lakh® printed forms and registers
lying at GP SAS Nagar were not lifted by the indenting departments even after
the period ranging from one to five years. Besides, 1.33 lakh printed forms
and registers valuing = 68 lakh lying at GP Patiala were also not delivered to
the indenting departments even after the period ranging between one and three
years.

The CP&S stated (October and December 2014) that the publications valuing
% 4.03 lakh had since been supplied to the indenting department and for the
disposal of other publications, etc. the matter had been taken up with the
concerned departments.

Thus, laxity on the part of the Department to dispose of old publications and
forms resulted in blockade of published material valuing हैं 1.58 crore besides
occupying useful space of GPs.

2.2.10.2 Defunct typewriter workshops

Government Typewriter Workshops (TW) were set up in 1964 with a view to
maintain and repair typewriters for Government/Semi Government
departments.

(0) Audit of records of CP&S (August 2014) showed that the Department,
considering the reduced workload on TWs owing to computerization in the
Government, semi-Government departments, decided (August 2010), with the
approval of minister-in-charge, to close 10 defunct TWs (out of 15) declaring
cadre of Typewriter Mechanics as diminishing cadre. The surplus staff was to
be adjusted against vacant posts (ministerial and technical) for productive use.

However, despite the orders of Minister-in-charge to close 10 TWs and retain
five TWs, the Department shifted (December 2011-June 2014) only
seven** TWs (including one at Ludhiana which was to be retained) to तिपा
TWs, but did not close/shift the remaining four’ TWs. Thus, the expenditure
of % 80 lakh on running of these workshops during 2011-14 was not justified
as the staff working in these four TWs could have been productively utilized
elsewhere in the Department as per the proposal (July 2010).

(ii) ‘It was observed from the information obtained from 15 TWs that
194828 typewriters were serviced by these TWs during 2009-14. But cross-
verification (August 2014) of service vouchers with three departments showed
that:

> While TW SAS Nagar showed to have repaired (between 7 January

office of Superintendent, Education Branch-III, Audit found only one
typewriter with the department.

> In the office of Secretary, P&S, Punjab, TW SAS Nagar claimed to
have repaired (between 13 January 2010 and 11 September 2013) up to

available in that office.

2.2.11 Internal control mechanism
2.2.11.1 Non-maintenance of records

The following records, as required under the provisions of Printing and
Stationery Manual and Accounting Procedure of the Department, were not
being maintained:

> As per Para 49 of Accounting Procedure, GPs SAS Nagar and Patiala
did not prepare their store accounts from 2005-06 and 2011-12 onwards,
respectively due to non-availability of overhead charges, as mentioned in
paragraph 2.2.8.3.

> As per Para 2.5 of P&S Manual, annual administrative report, which
inter alia included analysis of expenditure (direct and indirect) for working
out the percentage of overheads, was not prepared from 2004-05 onwards. In
its absence, neither the annual performance of the GPs at glance could be
monitored nor could the overheads be worked out, as mentioned in
paragraph 2.2.8.3.

> As per Para 81 of Accounting Procedure, register showing
department-wise work done in a year and cost thereof was not being
maintained. As a result thereof, department-wise printing cost of jobs done
could not be ascertained.

During exit conference, CP&S, while admitting the facts, assured to maintain
the requisite records to streamline the system.

Conclusions

The scheme for modernization of Government presses, despite provision of
funds, could not be implemented. Inadequate budgetary controls led to
preparation of unrealistic budget estimates and non-realization of receipts.
Despite budget provisions, the bills were not passed by treasury to clear
pending liabilities. Despite underutilizing the in-house printing capacity to the
extent of 61 per cent, 44 per cent of the printing orders were outsourced by the
Department. Out of 150 pending printing jobs as of March 2014, 112 jobs
pertained to the calendar years 2003-13. Non-working out the overhead
charges since 2004-05 affected the realization of actual cost of printing.
Procurement of stationery and paper was not in accordance with the definite
requirement. Efforts to dispose of excessive old publications, unserviceable
machines and material were lacking. Shortage of manpower affected the

39


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

optimum utilisation of the printing machines. Non-maintenance of important
records showed weak internal control and monitoring system of the
Department.

Recommendations

The State Government may:

>

> ensure timely execution of plans envisaged for modernization of printing
presses; take appropriate steps to prepare realistic budget estimates and
ensure timely release of funds; recover outstanding receipts; and review
the staff position;

> put in place a mechanism to monitor workload to optimally utilize
available infrastructure and augment printing capabilities so as to
minimize dependence on outsourcing;

> prepare annual administrative report to calculate overhead charges for
realistic job costing;

> ensure adoption of scientific method to assess the requirement of paper
and stationery well before its procurement, besides timely disposal of old
publications, unserviceable machinery, etc.; and

> strengthen internal control mechanism by ensuring maintenance of
important records.

The matter was referred to Government in September 2014; reply was awaited
(February 2015).

40


Chapter-2: Performance Audit

SCIENCE, TECHNOLOGY AND ENVIRONMENT DEPARTMENT
2.3 Management and disposal of bio-medical waste

Bio-Medical Waste (Management and Handling) Rules (BMW Rules)
prescribe the procedure for treatment and disposal of bio-medical waste
generated by Health Care Establishments (HCEs) such as hospitals,
dispensaries, nursing homes, diagnostic centres, veterinary institutions, etc.

Some of the significant findings of the performance audit are given below:

> Punjab Pollution Control Board (PPCB) did not have any
comprehensive list of Health Care Establishments (HCEs) functioning
in the State. PPCB had identified only 245 Government HCEs
(eight per cent) against 3176 Government HCEs functional in the State.
Sixty per cent of the identified HCEs were functioning without valid
authorisation.

(Paragraphs 2.3.6.1 & 2.3.6.2)

> Eighty per cent of test-checked Government HCEs did not have proper
arrangement for disinfection of liquid waste through chemical treatment
and the untreated liquid waste was being discharged in the drains
causing pollution to surrounding water bodies.

(Paragraph 2.3.8.4(iii))

> BMW was collected by Common Bio-medical Waste Treatment Facility
operator from 51 HCEs after a gap of up to 18 days.

(Paragraph 2.3.8.5)

> PPCB never inspected 39 test-checked Government HCEs to ascertain
proper treatment and disposal of BMW and 62 HCEs were visited after a
gap of up to nine years.

(Paragraph 2.3.10.1)

2.3.1 Introduction

Bio-medical waste (BMW) is generated during the diagnosis, treatment or
immunization of human beings or animals; or in research activities pertaining
thereto; or in the production or testing of biologicals. This waste is a reservoir
of potentially harmful micro-organisms which can infect hospital patients,
health care personnel and the general public. BMW management is an
integral part of health care.

Government of India, Ministry of Environment and Forests notified
(July 1998) the Bio Medical Waste (Management and Handling) Rules, 1998
(BMW Rules) under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986, to streamline the
procedure for handling, collection, transportation, treatment and disposal of
BMW with the objective of avoiding any adverse effect on human health and
environment. The BMW generating institutions were required to ensure

41


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

compliance with the provisions of BMW Rules within a time schedule
between 30 June 2000 and 31 December 2002 depending upon the category of
institution.

2.3.2 Organizational set up

The Secretary, Department of Science, Technology and Environment is the
administrative head at Government level for matters relating to environment.
Punjab Pollution Control Board (PPCB) headed by the Chairman, is the
prescribed authority in the State and is assisted by seven Zonal Offices and
14 Regional Offices (ROs) and Environment Protection Act (EPA) Cell to
enforce the provisions of BMW Rules in the State.

2.3.3 Audit objectives
The main objectives of the performance audit were to assess whether:

> all the BMW generating units in the State were identified and issued
authorisation to eligible Health Care Establishments;

> adequate funds were provided to Government Health Care
Establishments for disposal of bio-medical waste and utilized
effectively;

> BMW was handled, treated and disposed of in accordance with the
prescribed Rules/guidelines; and

> human resource was adequate and internal control mechanism was in
place.

2.3.4 Scope of audit and methodology

The performance audit covering the period 2009-14 was conducted between
October 2013 and June 2014. Seven districts*?(out of 22) were selected on the
basis of total in-patient days in Government Health Care Establishments
(HCEs) by adopting 'Probability Proportional to Size with Replacement!
method. In the selected districts, 105 Government HCEs~’ were selected with
‘Systematic Random Sampling’ method for test-check (Appendix-2.3). In
addition, records of EPA Cell of PPCB were also test-checked and related
information was obtained from Regional Offices of the selected districts,
Director Health Services (DHS), Punjab Health Systems Corporation (PHSC),
Director Research and Medical Education (DRME), Director, Animal
Husbandry (DAH) and Director, Employees State Insurance (ESI). Besides,
joint inspections along with the authorities of concerned establishments/PPCB
in respect of selected Government HCEs and four Common Bio-Medical

(i) Amritsar, (ii) Bathinda, (iii) Gurdaspur, (iv) Hoshiarpur, (v) Jalandhar, (vi) Ludhiana

and (vii) Tarn Taran,
(i) PHSC: all 7 District Hospitals and 19 Sub-Divisional Hospitals; and 23 Community
Health Centres (out of 69); (ii) DHS: 21 Primary Health Centres (out of 197); (iii) DHS
ESI: all 3 Hospitals and 22 Dispensaries (out of 38); (iv) DRME: all 3 Medical Colleges
and Hospitals; and (v) DAH: all 7 Veterinary Polyclinics.

Chapter-2: Performance Audit

Treatment Facilities*’ (CBWTF) under private sector situated in Punjab were
carried out. Information/data in respect of private HCEs collected from PPCB
has also been incorporated in the report. Mention was made in the Reports of
