31 March 2002 (paragraph 6.1.11.3) and 31 March 2011 (paragraph 2.1.14) on
bio-medical waste. Audit also examined the follow-up action by the concerned
departments, to the two paragraphs.

An entry conference was held with the Chairman, PPCB on 21 May 2014
wherein audit objectives, scope and methodology were discussed. The audit
findings were discussed in the exit conference held on 19 November 2014
with the Secretary to Government of Punjab, Department of Science,
Technology and Environment. Replies of the Department have suitably been
incorporated in the report.

2.3.5 Audit criteria
The audit findings were benchmarked against the criteria derived from the
following sources:

> Bio-Medical Waste (Management and Handling) Rules, 1998;

> Guidelines for installation of Common Bio-Medical Treatment
Facilities issued by Central Pollution Control Board;

> The Environment (Protection) Act, 1986; and

> Rules, orders and instructions issued by Central/State Government and

PPCB.
2.3.6 Identification and authorisation

2.3.6.1 Identification of Health Care Establishments

Rule 7 of BMW Rules, 1998 empowers PPCB to enforce the provisions of the
Rules in respect of all HCEs including hospitals, nursing homes, clinics,
dispensaries, veterinary institutions, etc.

Audit of records of PPCB showed that there was neither any system of State
level registration of HCEs nor any comprehensive list of HCEs (Government
and private) functioning in the State was available to ascertain proper
treatment of BMW generated by them. However, it was observed from the
information/data available with ROs that 3111 HCEs (Government: 245 and
private: 2866) were identified as on 1 April 2014. Whereas, as per
information obtained from the concerned Directorate health offices, 3176
Government HCEs” were operational in Punjab State as on 1 April 2014. Out
of 3176 Government HCEs, PPCB had identified only 245 (eight per cent)

(i)Amritsar; M/s Amritsar Envirocare Systems Pvt Ltd; (ii)Ludhiana: M/s Sembramky
Environment Management Pvt Ltd; (iii) Mohali: M/s Rainbow Environments Pvt. Ltd;
and (iv) Pathankot: M/s Bio-Medical Waste Treatment Plant Pvt. Ltd.

Comprehensive data bank on number of private HCEs was not available.

43


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

Government HCEs and the disposal of BMW by the remaining 2931
(92 per cent) Government HCEs was not ensured by PPCB.

During exit conference, the Secretary admitted the fact of non-availability of
data regarding total number of HCEs functional in the State owing to the
legislative gap for registration of HCEs and stated that ‘Punjab Health
Regulation Bill’, which would make it mandatory for all health care facilities
to register, was likely to be presented in the legislative assembly in the near
future.

2.3.6.2 Issue/Renewal of authorization

Rule 8 (1) provides that every occupier of an institution generating/handling
BMW in any manner, except such occupier of clinics, dispensaries,
pathological laboratories and blood banks providing treatment/services to less
than 1000 patients per month, shall make an application in Form-1 to the
prescribed authority for grant of authorisation. Further, Rule 7(5) provides
that an authorisation shall be granted for a period of three years from the date
of issue. Rule 7(7) provides that every application for authorisation shall be
disposed of by the prescribed authority within ninety days from the date of
receipt of the application. The Public Accounts Committee (PAC), while
discussing paragraph 6.1.11.3(i) of the Comptroller and Auditor General's
Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2002, had also desired to know
about the action taken by PPCB against the hospitals which had not applied
for authorization.

Audit, however, observed from the information/data available with ROs that
as on | April 2014, out of 3075 HCEs (Government: 244 and private: 2831)
eligible for authorization, 1839 (60 per cent) HCEs (Government: 169 and
private: 1670) were not having valid authorization for a period ranging up to
nine years.

In 105 test-checked Government HCEs, it was noticed that:

> 46 (63 per cent) out of 73 HCEs eligible for authorization did not have
valid authorization. Of these:

e 21 HCEs never applied for authorization;

e 13 HCEs did not apply for renewal of authorization even after delay
ranging between 60 and 677 days; and

e Though 12 HCEs applied for renewal (between March 2012 and
May 2014), the authorization was not granted by PPCB (August 2014). Of
these, seven HCEs** were not granted authorization even after a delay
ranging between 14 and 691 days over the prescribed time limit.

> Out of 27 HCEs found authorized, 13 HCEs provided records of
renewal of authorisation which showed that only two HCEs renewed their
authorization timely and 11 HCEs renewed their authorization with a delay
ranging between 16 and 907 days.

% Civil Hospital: (i) Jalandhar, (ii) Samrala; SDH: (iii) Nakodar, (iv) Khanna; and CHCs:
(v) Khemkaran, (vi) Adampur, (vii) Bandala.

44


Chapter-2: Performance Audit

The Secretary stated (November 2014) in the exit conference that 265 HCEs
had already been granted authorization and the remaining would also be
covered in due course. PPCB had also introduced Online Consent
Management and Monitoring System (OCMMS) for applying online for
authorization by HCEs through which report could be generated about HCEs
whose authorization had expired.

2.3.7 Financial management
2.3.7.1 Funding pattern

There is no separate allocation of budget for BMW management and disposal.
Expenditure for the purpose was being met out of various sources™ as
authorized by the concerned Directorates, which also cater to the other needs
of the HCEs. The lack of dedicated funds affected the disposal of BMW, as
discussed in paragraphs 2.3.8.1 and 2.3.8.4(iii).

The Secretary stated (November 2014) that HCEs under Punjab Health
Systems Corporation (PHSC) got sufficient funds for management and
disposal of BMW from ‘user charges’. As regards HCEs under other
Directorate offices, the matter would be taken up with the Government to
provide adequate funds for compliance of statutory provisions of the Act.

2.3.7.2 Non-fixation of rates to be charged from government HCEs by
CBWTF

As per guidelines for Common Bio-medical Waste Treatment Facility

(CBWTF), only one CBWTF may be allowed to cater to HCEs at the

approved rates fixed by the prescribed authority in consultation with the local

medical association in such a way that neither it becomes a monopoly of the

CBWTF operator nor the interest of CBWTF operator is overlooked.

PPCB in consultation with the authorities of local medical association fixed
(September 2011) the rates to be charged by the CBWTF operator in respect
of private HCEs. But, it did not fix rates for Government HCEs. In test-
checked districts, it was noticed that Dental College and Hospital, Amritsar
(10 bedded) entered into agreement with CBWTF, Amritsar at the rate of
% 6,000 per month (October 2011-September 2012) and % 13,250 per month
from October 2012 onwards, which was much higher than the rate of = 850
per month fixed (September 2011) by PPCB in respect of private Dental Care
and Hospitals (10 bedded) and was also in contravention of the guidelines
ibid.

The Secretary stated (November 2014) that earlier rates were fixed for private
HCEs only and Government HCEs followed the tendering process. It was
further added that PPCB had forwarded the revised rates in respect of private
HCEs to the Health Department to consider those at their level for
Government HCEs. As regards the matter of Dental College and Hospital,
Amritsar entering into an agreement at rates higher than the rates fixed for

5* TICEs under DHS: Rogi Kalyan Scheme under NRHM; HCEs under DHS (SI): SOE-
Material and Supply; HCEs under PHSC: User Charges; and HCEs under DRME: SOE-
Public Professional Social Services.

45


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

private HCEs, it was stated that the same were fixed taking into account the
number of dental chairs also. On being pointed out that there was no
provision in the orders issued by PPCB for considering the number of dental
chairs while fixing the rates, it was stated that the said orders would be
suitably amended.

2.3.8 Handling, treatment and disposal of bio-medical waste

(A) Health Care Establishments

2.3.8.1 Agreement with the Common Bio-Medical Waste Treatment
Facility

As per Rule 5, every occupier, where required, shall set up requisite BMW
facilities like incinerator, autoclave, microwave system for the treatment of
waste, or ensure requisite treatment of waste at a common waste treatment or
any other waste treatment facility. Since none of the HCEs in the State had set
up its own treatment facility, all the HCEs were required to enter into an
agreement with the respective CBWTF prescribed for that area for the
treatment and disposal of BMW. The Department apprised (March 2009)
PAC, while discussing paragraph 6.1.11.3(ii) of the Comptroller and Auditor
General's Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2002, that all the major
hospitals/HCEs had entered into agreement with CBWTF.

Audit, however, noticed that:

> In six®® out of 14 ROs, 372 HCEs (29 per cent) (Government:61 and
private: 311) out of 1280 HCEs (Government: 108 and private:1172) did not
have any agreement with the CBWTF of their respective areas. Remaining
eight®’ ROs did not supply the information (November 2014).

> Out of 105 test-checked Government HCEs, 39 HCEs (37 per cent) did
not have any agreement with CBWTFs for treatment and disposal of BMW.
Of these, eight’’ HCEs attributed the reasons for not having agreement with
CBWTF to lack of funds.

The Secretary attributed (November 2014) the lapse to shortage of staff and
stated that the situation would improve after recruitment of new staff by
December 2014. As regards, test-checked Government HCEs, it was stated
that the compliance by 35 HCEs had been made and in remaining four cases
legal action had been initiated.

(iii) Training to Staff

Clause 6 of the agreement made between HCE and CBWTF operator provides
that the initial training about segregation of BMW in colour plastic bags and
methods of collection of BMW shall be provided by the service provider
(CBWTEF) at no extra cost.

Audit noticed that in 66 test-checked HCEs having agreement with the
respective CBWTFs, training was imparted only in eight HCEs by CBWTF.

The Secretary stated (November 2014) that the Department was going to
impart dedicated training to staff for the purpose.

2.3.8.3 Storage of bio-medical waste

Para H-1 of the guidelines for CBWTF provides that temporary storage at
healthcare unit shall be designated. Further, Ministry of Environment and
Forests, Government of India instructed (2009) that PPCB should ensure that

48


Chapter-2: Performance Audit

BMW shall not be stored beyond 24 hours in hot and humid climate or shall
be stored in cold containers till it is transported to CBWTF.

Joint inspection in 105 test-checked Government HCEs showed that:

> Temporary storage room was not found in 69 HCEs (66 per cent). Of
these:

e In CHC Khemkaran and TB Hospital Amritsar, BMW was kept in
open area easily accessible to animals;

e In SDH Naushera Majha Singh, a small room adjacent to the labour
room was being used for keeping BMW; and

e In CHC Mahilpur and SDH Khanna, space at the middle portion of
stairs and mortuary, respectively was being used as temporary collection
room.

> Out of 36 Government HCEs where temporary collection room was
designated, in ESI Hospital Amritsar, BMW bags were found floating in the
containers full of water due to leakage in the water pipe passing through
collection room.

> None of the 105 test-checked Government HCEs was having cold
container to store BMW in hot and humid climate though in 45 HCEs delay
beyond 24 hours was noticed in hot and humid season.

The Secretary stated (November 2014) that it had been decided to initiate legal
action against the defaulters.

2.3.8.4 Treatment and disposal of BMW by HCEs

As per Rules, BMW is categorised into ten types according to the nature of
waste. The treatment/disposal options are incinerator, deep burial,
autoclaving, mutilation/shredding, microwaving, chemical treatment, secured
landfills, etc. depending upon the type of waste. Cases of improper treatment
and disposal of BMW in 105 test-checked Government HCEs are discussed in
the succeeding paragraphs.

(0) Deep burial pit

PPCB clarified (July 2013) that use of deep burial pits is strictly prohibited in
the State since March 2002.

It was noticed that despite ban on the use of burial pits, 11 HCEs were using
the deep burial method wherein all categories of BMW were being mixed with
general waste. Of these, in CHC Khemkaran and PHC Kum Kalan, the waste
was being burnt in deep burial pit from time to time though the former was
having an agreement with CBWTF.

49


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

ee

CHC Kum Kalan (23 April 2014)

CHC Khemkaran (24 December 2013)

The Secretary stated (November 2014) that all HCEs had been instructed to
enter into agreements with CBWTFs. He further stated that legal action would
be initiated against the HCEs using deep burial pits.

(ii) Mutilation/Shredding and chemical treatment

Schedule I of BMW Rules provides that categories 4 and 7 of BMW i.e. waste
sharps and solid waste should be disinfected by chemical treatment using at
least one per cent hypochlorite solution or any other equivalent chemical
reagent and mutilated/shredded to prevent its unauthorised reuse.

Audit noticed that in 105 selected Government HCEs:

> Waste sharp was not being disinfected in 45 HCEs due to non-
availability of hypochlorite solution, while in 10 HCEs, though the
hypochlorite solution was in stock, the waste sharp was not being disinfected
at all the generation points due to non-provision of hypochlorite solution at
those points.

> In 59 HCEs, at 116 generation points, the shredder for needles and
syringes either was not provided or was out of order.

> In 58 HCEs at 137 generation points, unshredded needles, syringes,
plastic glucose bottles, tubings, etc. were found. Of these, in PHC Sarhala
Kalan, large quantity of unshredded needles and syringes were lying for more
than six months and in ESI Dispensary Chohal, unshredded needles were lying
for more than two years.

The Secretary stated (November 2014) that show-cause notices had been
issued and legal action would be taken against the defaulters.

(iii) Treatment of liquid waste

As per Schedule V of BMW Rules, the effluent generated from hospitals
should conform to specified standards. These standards were applicable to
those hospitals which were either connected with sewers without terminal
sewage treatment plants or not connected to public sewers. To achieve
prescribed parameters Effluent Treatment Plant (ETP) should be installed.
HCEs which are connected with terminal sewage treatment plant should

50


Chapter-2: Performance Audit

disinfect liquid BMW by chemical treatment® before its discharge to public
drains.

Audit of records of PPCB showed that while 16 ETPs had been installed by
the private hospitals in the State, no Government HCE except one i.e. Guru
Gobind Singh Medical College and Hospital, Faridkot had installed ETP due
to shortage of funds, land etc. However, PPCB never took any sample of
liquid BMW from HCEs without ETP to ensure that the liquid waste was
disinfected properly before its disposal.

Test-check of 105 Government HCEs showed that out of 88 HCEs where
treatment of liquid waste was required, 70 HCEs (80 per cent) did not have
proper arrangement for disinfection through chemical treatment and untreated
liquid waste was being discharged directly in the drain. In CH Batala,
untreated liquid waste released in drain was accumulating in the hospital
campus as the sewerage was choked (28 May 2014). The discharge of
untreated liquid waste in drain carried a high risk of pollution of surrounding
water bodies.

The Secretary stated (November 2014) that show cause notices had been
issued and decided to take legal action against the defaulters.

(B) Common Bio-medical Waste Treatment Facilities

A Common Bio-medical Waste Treatment Facility (CBWTF) is set up in
private sector with the permission of PPCB where bio-medical waste,
generated from a number of HCEs, is imparted necessary treatment to reduce
adverse effects that the waste may pose. There are four’ CBWTF operators
for providing facilities for collection, treatment and disposal of BMW for all
the functional HCEs in Punjab.

2.3.8.5 Collection and transportation

Para H of the guidelines for CBWTF provides that BMW collected in colour
coded containers/bags shall be transported to the CBWTF in a fully covered
vehicle. The CBWTF shall not accept non-segregated waste from HCEs and

‘| PHCs- (i) Lehra Mohabbat; (ii) Khilchian; (iii) Sudhar; and (iv) Chamunda Devi.

© (i) CH-Raikot; (ii) Veterinary Polyclinic, Tarn Taran; (iii) CHC- Bucho Mandi; and
(iv) SDH-Ghudda.

(Amritsar: M/s Amritsar Envirocare Systems Pvt Ltd; (ii)Ludhiana: M/s Sembramky

and (iv) Pathankot: M/s Bio-Medical Waste Treatment Plant Pvt. Ltd.

list of such HCEs shall be communicated to PPCB every week. Further,
Rule 6(5) of BMW Rules provides that no untreated BMW shall be kept stored
beyond a period of 48 hours; and during hot and humid climate, the period of
storage shall be restricted to 24 hours, as per instructions of GOI.

A Joint inspection of four CBWTFs and 66 (out of 105) test-checked
Government HCEs having agreement with CBWTFs showed that:

> In 47 HCEs at 104 generation points, although BMW was not being
segregated properly yet CBWTF operators never intimated about this to
PPCB.

> In 51 HCEs, BMW was collected after a gap up to 18 days. In 45
HCEs, BMW was lifted beyond 24 hours in hot and humid climate. Five™

provided collection receipts of BMW for previous days also.

> In Veterinary polyclinic, Tarn Taran, serial numbers of hand receipts

not in consonance with dates which showed that receipts were not issued on
the same day of collection.

> In SDH Khanna, roof, sides and floor of the vehicle deployed for
collection of BMW by CBWTF Ludhiana was eroded by rust increasing the
possibility of spillage of liquid BMW, especially in rainy days.

> In SDH Khanna, CHC Noor Mahal and CH Patti, CBWTFs Ludhiana
and Amritsar collected BMW without weighing. In CH Patti, no weighing
machine/facility was available in the vehicle.

> White bags filled with blood soiled hand gloves and syringes (which
should have been in blue bags) without disinfection by HCEs were collected
by CBWTF Anritsar.

The Secretary viewed the irregularities seriously and stated (November 2014)
that show-cause notices had been served and bank guarantees encashed for the
lapses though not provided for in the Rules. It was further added that suitable
instructions would be issued to avoid such violations in future.

2.3.8.6 Operational capacity

CPCB guidelines for CBWTF provide that only one CBWTF may be allowed
to cater up to 10,000 beds and shall not be allowed to cater to healthcare units
situated beyond a radius of 150 km. The CBWTF would set up requisite
infrastructure i.e. separate housing for each of the treatment equipment, main
waste storage room, treated waste storage room, administrative room, parking
ete.

“ (i) Dental College Amritsar, (ii) CHC Bundala,(iii) Veterinary Polyclinic Ludhiana,

(iv) ESI Dispensary, Verka and (v) SDH Ghudda.

53


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

Audit examination showed (November 2013) that three (Amritsar, Ludhiana
and Mohali) out of four CBWTFs were catering to HCEs beyond a radius of
150 Km. CBWTF Ludhiana was also catering to 17500 beds. Further, the area
where washing of vehicles was to be done was fully occupied with containers
for BMW, in contravention of the guidelines.

The Secretary while admitting the facts attributed the reasons for the lapse to
inadequate number of CBWTFs in the State and added that to overcome the
capacity/area limitation violations, the Department had called for Expression
of Interest (EOI) for setting up of two more CBWTFs.

2.3.8.7 Treatment and disposal of BMW by CBWTFs

Joint inspection of four CBWTFs conducted along with PPCB authorities
showed the following discrepancies in treatment and disposal of BMW:

9 Incineration

Incineration is a controlled combustion process where waste is completely
oxidized and harmful micro-organisms present in it are destroyed/denatured
under high temperature. The temperature of the primary and secondary
chamber should be 800 + 50°C and 1050 + 50°C, respectively.

At CBWTF, Mohali, it was seen (3 January 2014) that temperature of
secondary chamber was not being displayed, in the absence of which it could
not be ascertained whether its temperature was within prescribed norms.
Category 7 of BMW (plastic glucose bottles, tubings, etc.) in blue bag to be
autoclaved was found with yellow bags in front of conveyer belt of incinerator
kept for incineration.

(ii) Autoclaving

As per Rules (Schedule I), BMW under categories 4 and 7 shall be disinfected
by chemical treatment/autoclaving/microwaving and mutilation/shredding.
However, as per Para E of the guidelines for CBWTF, the use of chemical
disinfection for the treatment of BMW at CBWTFs is not recommended.

It was noticed at CBWTF, Mohali that the autoclave was out of order since
3 December 2013 and BMW under categories 4 and 7 was being disinfected
manually by chemical treatment. Even when the autoclave was in order prior
to December 2013, only partial quantity was autoclaved and rest was manually
treated through chemical, in contravention of the guidelines.

During exit conference, it was stated that a show-cause notice was issued to
CBWTF (Mohali) and the bank guarantee had already been encashed. The
CBWTF had also been directed to avoid such violations in future.

hardware and software related problems being faced by CBWTF operators.
Thus, the objective of the Department to monitor efficient and timely
collection of BMW for better management as well as effective control over
CBWTFs through barcode-based online software remained unachieved.

2.3.9 Human resource management

At State level, only four personnel® had been deployed in EPA Section
(PPCB), whereas no dedicated staff was provided at regional level to monitor
the implementation of BMW Rules. In PPCB, against the sanctioned strength
of 665 posts, 239 posts (36 per cent) were lying vacant in different cadres as
on 31 March 2014. Shortage of staff led to lack of monitoring of
implementation of BMW Rules by HCEs, as discussed in the preceding
paragraphs.

The Secretary stated (November 2014) that necessary steps had already been
taken to fill up the vacant posts by December 2014.

2.3.10 Internal control mechanism

2.3.10.1_ Inspection

Rule 11 of BMW rules provides that all records maintained by HCEs were to
be subject to inspection and verification by PPCB at any time. The PPCB had
not fixed any norms for conducting inspection of HCEs and CBWTFs.
However, the advisory committee advised (November 2013) PPCB to conduct
inspection on quarterly basis.

No data with regard to inspection of HCEs and CBWTFs conducted by PPCB
was provided to Audit. Details of inspections conducted by PPCB during
2009-14, collected from nine ROs in respect of test-checked HCEs showed

Non-pasting of barcode stickers; and pasting of wrong colour and hospitals/CBWTFs
barcode stickers.

Additional Secretary, Environmental Engineer, Assistant Environmental Engineer, Junior
Environmental Engineer and Driver.

56


Chapter-2: Performance Audit

that 39 Government HCEs were never visited by PPCB and 62 HCEs were
visited after gap of up to nine years. Thus, lack of adequate inspections
affected the implementation of BMW Rules by HCEs, as discussed in the
preceding paragraphs.

The Department attributed this to shortage of staff and assured
(November 2014) that the issue regarding inadequate inspections would be
overcome after recruitment of staff by December 2014.

2.3.10.2 Monitoring
Audit noticed that:

> In spite of the directions (June 2011) of the advisory committee to
PPCB to visit all the veterinary hospitals, dispensaries and polyclinics to
collect data regarding quantity and category of BMW generated by these
institutions, no such data was collected by PPCB.

> Out of 105 test-checked HCEs, 87 HCEs (83 per cent) did not submit
their annual reports to PPCB in contravention to BMW Rule 10. As such, the
annual reports furnished by PPCB to CPCB were not containing authentic
data.

> Forty four test-checked HCEs were not maintaining records relating to
BMW generation and its handing over to CBWTF. Whereas 60 HCEs were
maintaining their records on the basis of collection slips supplied by CBWTFs.
Of these, only 39 HCEs were maintaining the log books of BMW. However,
the time of lifting (to ascertain prescribed time limit) by CBW’ was not
being mentioned in 34 log books and 27 log books were never signed by the
nodal officer or any other competent authority.

During exit conference, it was stated that they had also reviewed such cases
and show cause notices had been issued. It had also been decided to take legal
action against the defaulters. Further, it was also added that requisite
provision in the software had been made to enable the HCEs and PPCB to
generate daily, weekly and monthly reports.

2.3.10.3. Penalty

Section 15 of the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986 provides that failure to
comply with or contravention of any of the provisions of Environment Act,
shall attract penalty with imprisonment up to five years or fine up to
Zone lakh or both, and in case the failure or contravention continues, with
additional fine which may extend to % five thousand per day.

Information collected (July 2014) from PPCB showed that against
419 violations (Government: 48 and private: 371) noticed during 2009-13
(up to October 2013), PPCB invoked bank guarantee of हैं four lakh during
2009 and 2010 in two cases pertaining to CBWTF Pathankot though not
provided for under the Act/Rules. Of the remaining 417 cases:

57


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

>

> 200 cases (Government: 28 and private: 172) were decided without
taking any action being minor violations;

> warning was issued in 32 cases (Government: 2 and private: 30);

> prosecution was launched in 25 cases (Government: 14 and
private: 11); and

> bank guarantee was obtained in 160 cases (Government: 4 and
private: 156) though not provided for under the Rules.

The Secretary stated (November 2014) that there was no penal provisions in
the Rules under which PPCB would impose penalty. The matter would be
taken up with the Ministry of Environment and Forest, GOI to consider
amendment in the Rules to delegate some penal powers to the Department to
impose penalties in case of minor violations.

2.3.11 Impact of Audit

The Department stated (November 2014) that they had revisited the test-
checked 105 HCEs and four CBWTFs to review the extent of violations in
management and disposal of BMW and to initiate remedial action. It was
stated that show cause notices were served, personal hearings held and legal
action initiated in cases of serious violations. In a few cases, bank guarantees
of defaulters had been invoked. Further, PHSC has also formulated
(October 2014) eight-member district level committees to monitor the
management and disposal of BMW and issued instructions to all HCEs
highlighting the shortcomings pointed out in the performance audit for taking
remedial action.

Conclusions

Though Punjab Pollution Control Board (PPCB) was notified as statutory
authority responsible to enforce the implementation of bio-medical waste
(BMW) Rules, it did not have a comprehensive list of institutions generating
BMW in the State. As of March 2014, only eight per cent of the Government
Health Care Establishments (HCE) were brought under the purview of BMW
Rules and sixty per cent of HCEs had no valid authorization. Dedicated funds
were not provided to Government HCEs for proper management of BMW.
BMW was not properly segregated, stored and treated/disposed of by HCEs
and Common Bio-medical Waste Treatment Facilities (CBWTF) in
accordance with the prevalent Rules/guidelines. Human resource and internal
control mechanism was inadequate. In spite of being designated the oversight
body for ensuring compliance with BMW Rules, governing the treatment and
disposal of bio-medical waste in the State of Punjab, the extent and frequency
of PPCB's inspection of HCEs was low. There was lack of monitoring by
PPCB in the State as no checks were being exercised to see that waste
processing and disposal facilities met the compliance criteria outlined in the
BMW Rules.

58


Chapter-2: Performance Audit

Recommendations

The State Government may:

>

Vv

ensure identification of all the BMW generating HCEs in the State to
bring them under the purview of BMW Rules and issue authorisation
to eligible HCEs;

ensure availability of dedicated funds for proper management and.
disposal of BMW in Government sector;

ensure that BMW is handled, treated and disposed of by HCEs and
CBWTEs as per the prescribed Rules/guidelines; and

provide adequate manpower for close monitoring and inspections at
PPCB level, ensure adherence to the penal provisions under the
Act/Rules and proper maintenance of important records to strengthen
internal control mechanism in the Department for effective
implementation of BMW Rules.

The matter was referred to Government in August 2014; reply was awaited
(February 2015).

SOCIAL SECURITY AND WOMEN AND CHILD DEVELOPMENT
DEPARTMENT

2.4 Working of Social Security and Women and Child
Development Department

The Department of Social Security and Women and Child Development had
been providing services to the old and infirm, widows and destitute women
and women in moral danger, physically handicapped, blind, mentally retarded
children and children in need of care and protection.

Some of the significant findings of the performance audit are given below:

> Shortfall in coverage ranged between 50 and 53 per cent in case of
eligible children; between 23 and 26 per cent in case of pregnant
women and lactating mothers under Supplementary Nutrition
Programme; and between 51 and 59 per cent in Non-formal pre-
school education during the years 2009-14.

(Paragraphs 2.4.8.1(i&iv))

> Shortfall in supply of Medicine Kits was between 27 and 75 per cent
and of Pre-school Education kits between 20 to 74 per cent.

(Paragraphs 2.4.8.1 (ii&iii))

> Funds of €37.05 crore under four schemes were either not released
or partially released by the Finance Department/treasury despite
availability of budget provisions.

(Paragraphs 2.4.8.2(i), 2.4.8.3 (iii),2.4.8.6&2.4.8.7)

> Facilities of drinking water and toilets were not available in 1524
(15 per cent) and 4181 (42 per cent) Anganwadi Centres respectively
in the five test-checked districts.

(Paragraph 2.4.8.2(ii))

> Under ‘Awareness against Drug Abuse Scheme’, the Department
could organize only 41 (against 260) theme camps at District/Block
level; 52 (against 410) TV campaigns; 418 (against 2031) radio

campaigns, etc. during 2010-14.
(Paragraph 2.4.8.5)

> No budget provisions were made under Information Technology for
braille literacy in Indian Languages Scheme for the last eight years.

(Paragraph 2.4.8.8)

2.4.1 Introduction

The Department of Social Security and Women and Child Development
(SSWCD) had been providing services to the old and infirm, widows and
destitute women and women in moral danger, physically handicapped, blind,

60


Chapter-2: Performance Audit

mentally retarded children and children in need of care and protection. To
fulfil the objectives of the department, Government of India (GOI) and
Government of Punjab (GOP) had been implementing 39 schemes in the
Punjab during 2009-14.

2.4.2 Organizational set up

Principal Secretary to the GOP, SSWCD is the administrative head of the
Department. The Director, SSWCD is the overall in-charge for
administration, coordination, implementation and monitoring of the schemes
(both Centrally and State sponsored) and is assisted by Additional Director,
Joint Director, Deputy Controller (Finance and Accounts) and Deputy
Directors at the Directorate Level, District Social Security Officers (DSSOs),
District Programme Officers (DPOs) and Superintendent Homes at district
level and Child Development Project Officers (CDPOs) at block level.

2.4.3 Audit objectives

The Performance audit was conducted to assess whether:

> adequate planning for implementation of welfare measures was in
place and effective;

Vv

the financial management was effective and adequate;

Vv

the infrastructure and human resources were adequate; and

हि

internal control system was in place.

2.4.4 Scope of Audit and Methodology

The Performance Audit for the period 2009-14 was conducted from
November 2013 to July 2014 by test checking the records relating to nine
selected schemes® out of 39 schemes in the offices of Director, SSWCD,
DPOs, DSSOs, CDPOs and Superintendents, Homes. Out of 20 districts, five
districts” (25 per cent) were selected on the basis of expenditure incurred
under the selected schemes in the districts by adopting Probability
Proportional to Size with Replacement Method.

An entry conference was held in March 2014 with the Principal Secretary,
SSWCD to know about the implementation of various schemes/programmes
and other activities of the Department as well as to explain the scope,
objectives, plan, methodology and time schedule for conducting the
Performance Audit. Audit findings were discussed with the Special Secretary
to the Government of Punjab, Department of SSWCD, in an exit conference
held in November 2014. Responses received from the departments concerned
have suitably been incorporated in the report.

2.4.5 Audit criteria
The audit criteria was based on the following sources:

> Provisions contained in the Punjab Budget Manual, Punjab Financial
Rules and instructions issued by State Government/GOI from time to
time;

> Guidelines of the schemes;

> Norms prescribed for manpower requirement and its skill upgradation
by the Department; and

» Guidelines issued by the Finance Department for internal control
mechanism.

2.4.6 Planning

Proper planning and survey of beneficiaries are imperative and a pre-requisite
for achieving the objectives of programmes/schemes. The Department was to
establish model Anganwadi Centres (AWCs), construct Homes for Children in
need of care and protection, cover beneficiaries below six years under
Supplementary Nutrition Programme (SNP), provide non-formal Education,
Health and Nutrition Services and provide basic amenities in the AWCs,
create awareness among the masses about the ill effects of drug usages and to
create awareness among the rural masses about domestic violence Act to
liberate women of various kinds of domestic violence.

Audit of records showed that the Department had not prepared any long term
comprehensive plan to achieve its objectives. In the exit conference, Special
Secretary, SSWCD stated that planning was done on yearly basis as it was
difficult to prepare medium or long term perspective plan due to uncertainty
about availability of funds. The reply was not convincing as non-preparation
of perspective plans had resulted in improper implementation of various
schemes.

2.4.8 Implementation of the schemes:

Out of 39 schemes implemented by the department, 9 schemes were selected
for the Performance Audit, important findings of which are given below.

2.4.8.1 Integrated Child Development Services scheme

The Integrated Child Development Services scheme (ICDS), a centrally
sponsored scheme, was started in 1975-76 in the State with a holistic
approach to provide integrated services for the development of children and to
enhance the capability of the mother to look after the normal health and
nutritional needs of the child. Under this scheme, six services” are provided to
children in the age group of 0-6 years and to pregnant women and lactating
mothers. Funds of हैं 1636.94 crore were allotted during 2009-14 under this
scheme against which an expenditure of = 1300.11 crore was incurred as of
March 2014.

(9 Shortfall in the coverage of beneficiaries under Supplementary
Nutrition Programme

As per Para 15 of the ICDS guidelines, Central Assistance for Supplementary

Nutrition under the ICDS scheme was to be provided for 300 days in a year to

all the children of 6 months to 6 years, to pregnant women and lactating
mothers.

Audit of records (June 2014) of Director SSWCD showed short coverage of
eligible beneficiaries under Supplementary Nutrition Programme (SNP)
during 2009-14 as given in the Table 2.4.3.

Chapter-2: Performance Audit

From the table it is evident that the shortfall in the coverage of eligible
children ranged between 50 and 53 per cent whereas in case of pregnant
women and lactating mothers it was between 23 and 26 per cent.

Audit of records (November 2013-June 2014) of DPOs in the selected
districts showed that the coverage of eligible beneficiaries under SNP during
the years 2009-14 ranged between 46 and 49 per cent in respect of children
and between 18 and 38 per cent in case of pregnant women and lactating
mothers.

In the exit conference (November 2014), the Department admitted the fact and
stated that shortfall was due to shortage of funds.

(ii) Short supply of Medicine Kits

As per GOI, Ministry of Human Resources Development (MoHRD),
Department of Women and Child Development (DoWCD) instruction
(March 2000) as a vital input to provide essential services of health check up
and referral services, each AWC was to be provided with a medicine kit each
year consisting of easy to use dispensable medicines for common ailment like
cough, common cold, skin infections, etc.

Audit of records (November 2013 —June 2014 ) in selected districts showed
that against the requirement of 42193 medicine kits, only 18722 kits were
supplied during the period 2009-14 resulting in shortfall between 27 and 75
per cent. Thus, the supply of medicine kits, which is an important component
under the ICDS was not ensured resulting in deprival of intended benefits to
the beneficiaries.

(iii) — Short Supply of Pre School Education Kits

As per GOI, MoHRD, DoWCD instruction (March 2000), Pre-School
Education (PSE) component under the ICDS scheme is a crucial component to
ensure holistic development of children. Recognizing the significance of early
child development in attainment of the goal of Universal Elementary
Education, GOI decided to improve the quality of education in AWCs through
a new initiative of regular provision of PSE Kits for each AWC. The PSE in
AWCs is provided through non-formal and play way methods.

Test check of records (November 2013—June 2014) of 48 blocks in selected
districts showed that against the requirement of 42193 kits, as per the norms of
minimum one kit per centre per year, only 22336 kits were supplied during the
period 2009-10 to 2013-14 resulting in shortfall between 20 and 74 per cent.

In the exit conference, while admitting the fact, the Department stated
(November 2014) that shortfall in supply of Medicine and Education kits was
due to non-release of funds in time by FD and delay in finalization of rate
contract.

(7). Non-formal pre-school education

As per Para 23 of the ICDS guidelines, Children between 3-6 years of age
were to be provided non-formal PSE through AWCs to develop desirable

65


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

attitudes, values and behaviour pattern among them. The target for enrolment
under PSE was 100 per cent.

Audit of records (July 2014) of Director, SSWCD, showed that PSE was not
provided to all the children in the age group 3-6 years. There was shortfall in
providing PSE ranging between 51 and 59 per cent.

In the exit conference, the Department stated (November 2014) that ICDS was
a voluntary scheme and it was not mandatory to reach an unwilling target
group. The reply is not tenable because hundred per cent coverage was to be
achieved. Hence, more efforts were required to achieve the target.

(o) Nutritional status of children

As per Appendix-B (iii) of the ICDS Guidelines, to detect diseases and
evidence of malnutrition or infection, etc., general check-up of all children
under the age of six years was to be done after every three to six months. A
close watch over their nutritional status was also to be kept by recording their
height and weight and referring serious cases to appropriate hospitals for
specialised treatment.

The nutritional status of children examined on the basis of weight
measurement at the AWCs in the test checked districts during 2009-14 showed
that the percentage of malnourished children had shown a declining trend and
came down from 35 to 25 per cent during the years 2009-14 though no targets
were fixed by the Department.

(vi) _ Physical monitoring of quality of food

The GOI, MoWCD guidelines (October 2010) for monitoring and supervision
visits to ICDS Blocks and AWCs provide that officials from the field units of
Food and Nutrition Board (Board) under the MWCD should visit 10 AWCs
per month or as prescribed, whichever is more, to be equally distributed across
all districts in the State.

Test check of records (July 2014) and information supplied by the Board
showed that officials of the Board, Ludhiana had visited the Anganwadi
Centres (AWCs) for physical monitoring of quality of food all over the Punjab
State during 2009-14 and collected the food samples. It was noticed that 414
AWCs were only visited against the target of 600 AWCs during 2009-14
resulting in shortfall of 31 per cent. During 2009-14 Board covered 3 to 7
districts out of 20 districts of the State resulting in non-coverage of other
districts. Further, the food samples of ready to eat item Panjiri annually
collected from only 73 AWCs were sent to Regional Headquarter at New
Delhi for estimation and analysis. No report from that office had been
received, in the absence of which the quality of food could not be verified in
audit. However, the GOP did not take up the matter with GOI at any stage for
monitoring of quality of food as per the prescribed norms.

In the exit conference, the department stated (November 2014) that the matter
would be taken up with the authorities of the Board (Field Unit).

66


Chapter-2: Performance Audit

(vii) Shortfall in Inspection/field visits

The GOI, MoWCD, issued guidelines (October 2010) for monitoring and
supervision through visits to ICDS Blocks to provide necessary support to the
ICDS field functionaries in improving the quality of service delivery by
addressing various problems/bottlenecks and also to elicit views and
perspective from the community for improvement in day-to-day functioning of
AWCs. Intensive monitoring and supervision visits by Programme Officials at
different levels are essential for taking appropriate corrective actions.

For this purpose, a schedule for State/District/Block level functionaries was
framed, to ensure coverage of at least 20 AWCs in each quarter and
10 per cent of blocks every year by the State Director, at least 10 AWCs and
two blocks in each month by Nodal Officer and at least five AWCs in each
month and 20 per cent of blocks every year by the Joint Director/Deputy
Director.

The norms of field visits /inspections fixed for CDPOs is 20 AWCs per month
on rotational basis and to ensure 100 per cent coverage in a year and in the
case of Supervisors, a minimum of 50 per cent of AWCs under his jurisdiction
every month. Against 1300 AWCs to be visited, only 258 AWC were visited
by the Directorate level functionaries and as per information made available
by the Department no field visits to blocks were made by the State Director.

Further, CDPOs and Supervisors had not conducted inspection/field visits to
the AWCs as per norms. Shortfall of inspections ranged between 58 and 69
per cent by the CDPOs and between 44 and 56 per cent by the Supervisors
during the period 2009-14.

In the exit conference (November 2014), the Department stated that due to
additional charge of the Director and shortage of State Level Officer in ICDS,
CDPOs and supervisors, the required number of visits could not be conducted.

2.4.8.2 Infrastructure/basic amenities for Anganwadi Centres in the
State

The Anganwadi centres (AWCs) cater to the needs of children in the age
group of 6 months to 6 years and women in the reproductive age of 15-45
years. In order to provide all the child friendly facilities to the children, it is
necessary to have own buildings as facilities could not be provided in the
buildings like Dharamshalas, Gurudwaras, etc., where at present these
Anganwadi Centres are functioning. For construction of AWC building, the
land would be provided by the Panchayats. In order to provide clean and
healthy environment to the beneficiaries, ¥ 38 crore were allotted under the
scheme — ‘Infrastructure/basic amenities for Anganwadi Centres in the State’
during the years 2011-14 by the GOP for construction of 850 AWCs. Test
check of records showed the following:

(0) Construction of Anganwadi Centre buildings

The AWC is the focal point where the services under different schemes of
Centre/State Government are to be delivered. Test check of records

67


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

(November 2013-June 2014) of 48 blocks showed that out of 8471 operational
AWCs in the selected districts, 178 AWCs were functioning in own buildings;
1271 AWCs in rented buildings; 6905 AWCs in community buildings; and
117 AWCs in open spaces during the year 2013-14. In order to provide clean
and healthy environment to the beneficiaries, ¥ 38 crore were allotted during
the years 2011-14 by the GOP for construction of 850 AWCs in different
blocks under the scheme. The expenditure was to be shared” between GOI
and GOP.

Against the total allocation of = 38 crore, हैं 4.59 crore only were released to
the DPOs” for construction of 114 AWCs. No funds were released during
2013-14. Further, it was noticed that no new AWCs was constructed out of the
funds withdrawn during 2011-13. The funds of हैं 4.59 crore were lying
unutilized with the Department resulting in continuation of the AWCs in
improper environment besides blockade of funds.

In the exit conference, the Department stated (November 2014) that status of
utilization of funds and construction work of AWC buildings would be
supplied shortly but the same was still awaited (December 2014).

It is, thus, evident that the AWCs were forced to function in improper
environment for the tender aged children thereby adversely affecting their
development and the Government had not taken remedial measures by
constructing new buildings despite availability of Central funds.

(ii) Inadequate basic facilities in the Anganwadi Centres

As per Para 3.2, Chapter-3 of ICDS Manual the facility of safe drinking water
and hygienic sanitary conditions in the AWCs is an essential element of ICDS
Scheme. It was, however, noticed that though the ICDS Scheme was being
implemented in the State since 1975-76 yet inadequacy in facilities still
persisted in the AWCs as of June 2014.

Audit of records (November 2013 to June 2014) of selected districts showed
that out of 9922 AWCs, drinking water facility in 1524 AWCs (15 per cent)
and toilet facilities in 4181 AWCs (42 per cent) were not available.

In the exit conference, the department stated (November 2014) that due to
non-provision of funds by the FD the requisite basic facilities could not be
provided in the AWCs. The fact remains that the department failed to provide
the basic facilities in the AWCs even after a lapse of more than 38 years of
inception.

2.4.8.3 Enforcement of Juvenile Justice Act 2000 and Integrated Child
Protection Scheme

The Juvenile Justice (Care and Protection of Children) Act, 2000 was enacted
with the basic objective of providing justice to children in conflict with law and

children in need of care and protection by adopting child friendly approach and
to rehabilitate them keeping in view their development needs. The scheme was
merged (2011-12) with the Integrated Child Protection Scheme (ICPS). Funds of
हैँ 76.53 crore were allotted during 2009-14 under this scheme against which an

(0) Shortfall in occupancy against sanctioned capacity in Homes

The Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment, GOI, issued (January 2003)
basic guidelines for the maintenance/upkeep/construction of the Children
Homes/State After Care Homes set up under the Scheme “A home for juvenile
justice” in every district or in group of districts as required. Eleven such
Homes were functioning in the eight districts of Punjab for providing shelter
to the children of deprived sections of society.

Audit of records (July 2014) of Director SSWCD showed that Children
Homes/State After Care Homes remained under utilized during the years
2009-14 as the occupancy was in the range of 221 to 241 (37 per cent to 40
per cent) against the sanctioned capacity of 600 inmates, primarily due to
insufficient publicity about the scheme.

In the exit conference, Special Secretary stated (November 2014) that efforts
would be made to create more awareness among people through press, media,
etc. for enrolment of needy children.

(ii) Irregular functioning of Children Homes/State After Care Home

Under Rule 36 of Punjab Juvenile Justice (Care and Protection of Children)
Rules 2014, the homes for juvenile in conflict with law and children in need of
care and protection, shall function from separate premises. Further, After Care
Organisation shall be available for people between 18-21 years of age, who
have no place to go and are unable to support themselves.

In two, out of six homes in five selected districts, audit of records (April-
May 2014) showed that out of 57 inmates, 12 inmates, in conflict with law and
with court cases pending against them, were accommodated in Children
Homes for Girls at Jalandhar whereas these inmates were required to be
accommodated in Observation Homes. Similarly, two inmates in conflict with
law and 14 inmates above 21 years of age were also residing in the State After
Care Home, Amritsar in contravention of the rule. The retention of inmates in
conflict with law was not only against the rules but could also adversely
impact the mind set of other children residing with them in the Homes.

In the exit conference, Special Secretary stated (November 2014) that inmates
in conflict with law, would be shifted to observation homes shortly.

(iii) Non-Construction of Observation and Children Homes

The Ministry of Woman and Child Development (MWCD), GOI sanctioned
(August 2011) grant-in-aid of $119.44 lakh (® 89.58 lakh as Central share and
% 29.86 lakh as State share in the ratio of 75:25) for construction of
Observation Home and Children Home at Faridkot and Patiala respectively.

69


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

Though the GOI released (2011-12) funds of हैं 89.58 lakh for construction of
the Homes, the work could not be started due to non-release of State share of
% 29.86 lakh. It is also pertinent to mention that despite the fact that children
of Observation Homes were being accommodated in Children Homes in
violation of the rules, at Jalandhar and Amritsar (as discussed in Para
2.4.8.3(ii)), the GOP had not utilized the Central assistance for the said
purpose and was lying in the saving bank account.

In the exit conference, the Department stated (November 2014) that due to
non-release of State share, construction work of Homes could not be started.

Thus, it is evident that due importance/priority to this issue was not given by
the Government and as such the much needed Observation/Children Homes
could not be constructed.

(iv) Non-formation of Home Management Committee and non-
conducting of meetings of Child Welfare Committee

As per guidelines of the ICPS issued by the MWCD, GOI every institution
shall constitute a Home Management Committee (HMC) chaired by the
Assistant Director Child Protection from the District Child Protection Unit to
ensure effective functioning of the institution.

Audit of records (April 2014—June 2014) of State After Care Homes, Ludhiana
and Amritsar, Observation Home Ludhiana and Children Home, Jalandhar
showed that HMC was not constituted. Further, audit of records (June 2014)
of State After Care Home and Observation Home, Ludhiana showed that no
meeting was held though CWC was constituted in July 2011.

In the exit conference (November 2014), the Department stated that HMCs
had been constituted in 12 districts and in the remaining districts it would be
constituted shortly and efforts would be made to conduct the meetings of
CWC as per norms.

Thus, the quality of services delivered to the inmates could not be ensured and
their grievances could not be redressed.

2.4.8.4 Indira Gandhi Matritiva Sehyog Yojana

Indira Gandhi Matritiva Sehyog Yojana (IGMSY) a Conditional Maternity

being implemented on pilot basis in Amritsar and Kapurthala districts. Under
the scheme, a cash incentive of *ैं 4000 is to be provided to women of 19 years
and above for the first two live births. The payment is made to the
beneficiaries directly in their bank accounts. Further, cash incentive of = 200
and हैं 100 is also provided to Anganwadi Worker (AWW) and Anganwadi
Helper (AWH) respectively after all the cash transfers to the beneficiaries
bank accounts are completed. Funds of % 40.87 crore were allotted during
2009-14 under this scheme against which an expenditure of € 9.82 crore was
incurred as of March 2014.

2.4.8.5 Awareness against Drug Abuse Scheme

The scheme was formulated in 2005 with the objective to create awareness
against drug abuse among the people through Electronic and Print media,
counselling in school/colleges and theme camps in villages, blocks and
districts.

The funds of हैं 0.98 crore (24 per cent) were only released/utilized against
the total budget allotment of हैं 4.14 crore during 2010-14. The
Department could organize only 41 (against 260) theme camps at
District/Block level; 52 (against 410) TV campaigns; 418 (against 2031)
radio campaigns, etc. during 2010-14. However, during 2009-10, no
expenditure was incurred under the scheme.

In the exit conference, the Department stated (November 2014) that due to
non-release of funds by the Finance Department (FD), the targeted awareness
among the masses about the ill effects of drugs could not be created. Thus,
despite provision of funds and the fact that the drug abuse is a serious problem
in the State, the State Government failed to provide due importance to the
problem.

2.4.8.6 Awareness Programme for Domestic Violence Act, 2005

The Scheme was formulated in 2008-09 to liberate women of various kinds of
domestic violence by creating awareness among the rural masses about the
said Act and penal provisions involved through print and electronic media,
street plays, skits, seminars, speeches, etc.

Against the total budget allotment of ¥ 1.75 crore during 2009-13, an amount
of हैं 0.30 crore (17 per cent) was only released/utilized on 24 seminars at
District/Block level and awareness through TV and Radio medium during
2010-11 and 2012-13, Further, no funds were allotted during the year 2013-14.
The department failed to organize other planned activities such as street plays,
skits, etc.

In the exit conference, the Department stated (November 2014) that due to
non-release of funds by FD, targeted activities could not be carried out.
2.4.8.7 Setting up of Community Homes for Mentally ill Persons Scheme

The scheme was launched in 2011-12 with the objective to provide financial
assistance to Non-Government Organizations (NGOs) for establishing

71


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

protected community homes for those mentally ill/retarded persons who were
discharged from prison and no longer require full time care for mental illness,
but are unable to take care of themselves.

A budget allotment of one crore during the period 2009-14 was made under
this scheme. However, no expenditure was incurred by the Department under
the scheme.

In the exit conference, the Department stated (November 2014) that the
scheme was launched in 2011-12 to be run through NGOs. But no NGO
volunteered to setup such home, so the budget provided under this scheme
could not be utilized. The fact remains that despite availability of funds, the
mentally ill persons were deprived of the intended benefits.

2.4.8.8 Information technology for Braille Literacy in Indian
Languages Scheme

The scheme was launched in 2005-06 with the objective to develop
educational infrastructure for the blind through application of information
technology, to develop technology for dissemination of educational material
and information in Indian languages, to develop an electronic library and to
establish source centre, training centres and networking.

The scheme could not be implemented at all and remained on paper only as
neither any funds were allocated under the scheme nor any discussion
regarding implementation of the scheme was held with the Government at any
stage.

In the exit conference, the Department stated (November 2014) that the
scheme was not implemented since the last eight years as no budget had been
allocated under the scheme. Thus, the objective of the scheme stood totally
defeated.

2.4.9 Human Resource Management

The Director, Women and Child Development is the overall-in-charge for
implementation of the schemes. He is assisted by the Programme Officer,
District Social Security officer, Superintendent Homes at the district level and
CDPOs at the Block level. Field level functionaries comprising AWWs,
Supervisors, CDPOs, POs, DSSOs and Superintendent Homes play an
important role in implementation of the schemes.

Department during the period 2009-14 is given in Table 2.4.4.

There was a shortage of staff ranging between 30 and 38 per cent in the

2.4.9.2 Training

As per Para 51 of ICDS guidelines, CDPOs, Supervisors, AWWs and AWHs
are required to be trained for the tasks assigned to them in order to improve
quality of services to be provided by them.

There was a shortfall in imparting training to the staff in selected districts (as
on 31.03.2014) as against target of imparting training to 7275, only 6415
AWHs were trained (March 2014). Further, training was not imparted to eight
CDPOs, 19 Supervisors and 68 AWWs. In the absence of trained staff,
delivery of quality services to the beneficiaries could not be ensured.

In the exit conference, the department stated (November 2014) that training
was an on-going process and would be imparted to the untrained staff.

2.4.10 Internal Control Mechanism

The Director was responsible for monitoring and evaluation of implementation
of the programmes, schemes and other activities of the department. However,
it was noticed that:

> The periodical reports of each programme/scheme which were
submitted to the Director SSWCD by the Controlling Officers in the field
were merely compiled at Directorate level but no corrective measures were
being taken to improve the working of the department.

> No Internal Audit Wing of the department was functioning at
Directorate level as FD entrusted with the responsibility of internal audit of
accounts kept in the subordinate offices.

> Inadequate field inspections were conducted by the Directorate level
functionaries, CDPOs and Supervisors.

> Quality of Services related to Children Home, State After Care Home
and Observation Homes in the State could not be ensured due to non-

73


Audit Report—Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

constitution of Home Management Committees and non-conducting of
meetings of Child Welfare Committees.

In the exit conference, the Department stated (November 2014) that internal
audit of the Department was required to be conducted by Internal Audit Wing
of the Finance Department. But due to shortage of staff Internal Audit wing of
the Department was not functional during 2009-14.

Conclusions

It was evident that despite making provisions in the budget estimates and
release of central share by the Government of India, funds were either not
released by the Finance Department or by the treasuries with the result social
welfare schemes suffered. There was shortfall in coverage of beneficiaries
under SNP and Pre-school education. Under Health and Nutritional status, the
proportion of malnourished children had come down. The department failed to
provide minimum basic facilities of safe drinking water, toilets etc. in large
number of AWCs. The AWCs/Children Homes/Observations Home could not
be constructed despite availability of Central funds. Due to lack of public
awareness, the occupancy of existing capacity in Children Homes/State After
Care Homes was very low. Though Drug Abuse and Domestic Violence are
serious problems in the State, the requisite awareness could not be created due
to non-availability of timely and adequate funds. The schemes for setting up
community homes for mentally ill persons and Information Technology for
Braille Literacy in Indian Languages could not be implemented at all due to
non-release of funds by the State Government. Staff availability at field levels
was not adequate. Physical monitoring of quality of food was not done as per
norms, as visits and samples of food were not taken by the officials of
Nutrition Board. The monitoring at Directorate level/field level was not
adequate and internal audit was not functional.

Recommendations
The Government may:

> ensure timely release of Centre/State funds by Finance
Department/treasury as per budget provisions;

Vv

ensure construction of Anganwadi Centres, much needed
Observation/Children Homes, setting up of Community Homes for
Mentally ill Persons and to provide basic amenities, in all the
Anganwadi Centres;

> provide adequate funds for the sensitive issues like Awareness of Drug
Abuse, Domestic Violence and Child Protection; Information
Technology for Braille Literacy in Indian Languages; and

> strengthen internal control mechanism — monitoring, inspection and
internal audit.

The matter was referred to Government in August 2014; reply was awaited
(February 2015).

Compliance Audit



3.1 Purchase of helicopter in a non-transparent manner

Rule 15.2 (b) of the Punjab Financial Rules (PFR), Volume-I provides that
when stores are purchased from the open market, the system of open
competitive tender should, as far as possible, be adopted as prescribed in
Appendix 8 of PFR, Volume-II (Appendix). Complete specifications and
drawings of the stores to be purchased should be prepared by the indenting
department (Rule 4 of the Appendix), on the basis of which open tenders are
to be invited (Rule 7 and Rule 8 of the Appendix). Tenders in response to such
bid invitations should be submitted in a sealed cover (Rule 9 of the Appendix),
which are then evaluated against the prescribed specifications.

Test check of records of the Director, Civil Aviation, Punjab
(November 2013) showed that in disregard to the rules ibid, without any
formal enquiry by the Government of Punjab (GOP), four! Helicopter
manufacturing firms submitted unsolicited and suo-moto proposals for
different makes of helicopter at different points of time (between August 2009
and April 2012) through their representatives and agents. It was further
noticed that the Civil Aviation Minister constituted (4 April 2012) a
Committee under the Chairmanship of Chief Secretary, Punjab (CS) to look
into the modalities of procuring a helicopter by the Department for use of the
VVIPs. The Committee after examining (10 April 2012) the available
proposals recommended that the Bell 429 Helicopter be considered for final
decision in the meeting of the Cabinet Sub-Committee on Civil Aviation
Affairs. The Cabinet Sub-Committee of Civil Aviation under the chairmanship
of Chief Minister, Punjab met and granted (10 April 2012) its post facto
approval for seeking quotations from the sole authorized suppliers/agents of
manufacturers of helicopter companies and accorded its approval to purchase
one Bell 429 helicopter and directed CS to negotiate price with the agent. A
purchase agreement for purchase of Bell 429 helicopter was made
(5 July 2012) with M/s. Bell Helicopter Asia (PTE) Limited. The delivery of
the helicopter was accepted in November 2012 at a total cost of US Dollar
6689350 (approximately हैं 36.62 crore’). Thus, the Department purchased the
helicopter in disregard to the rules ibid and without any pre-defined
specifications, etc.

The Adviser Civil Aviation in his reply stated (May 2014) that purchase had
been made as per the selection and procurement procedure approved by the

1 Agusta Westland, Italy; Eurocopter, France; Bell Textron, Canada; and Hawker Beech

Craft Corporation, USA.

? 1 US Dollar=% 54.7372 as on 17 December 2012.


Audit Report-Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

committee constituted under the chairmanship of CS and the same committee
had approved the submission of proposals and quotes by the interested parties.
The Adviser, Civil Aviation in a subsequent reply (August 2014) stated that
the selection of the helicopter was done after thorough comparison of the
available machines with no loss to the State exchequer. The replies were not
acceptable as the purchase of the helicopter was finalized by the Cabinet Sub-
Committee of Civil Aviation in disregard to the rules prescribed for public
procurement and in a non-transparent manner.

Evidently, the purchase procedure adopted by the Department was violative of
the provisions of the PFR as the proposals so received were not in response to
pre-defined detailed technical specifications, quality bench marks and definite
requirement of GOP, and these were received at different points of time for
different types of Helicopters and hence were not financially or technically
comparable to determine whether the most economical and appropriate
proposal was chosen. Thus, procurement of helicopter valuing हैं 36.62 crore
was not in accordance with the extant Rules.

work shall be commenced unless allotment of funds is made and orders for its

3.4 Avoidable burden of interest on State exchequer

Rule 2.10(a) of Punjab Financial Rules Volume-I provides that every
Government employee incurring or sanctioning expenditure from the revenues
of the State should be guided by high standards of financial propriety and is
expected to exercise the same vigilance in respect of expenditure incurred
from public money as a person of ordinary prudence would exercise in respect
of the expenditure of his own money.

Test check of records (March 2014) of the Director, Punjab State Planning
Board showed that the Estate Officer of Union Territory (UT), Chandigarh
(EO) offered (9 February 2007) land measuring 2.58 acre to GOP in Sector 38
at a tentative cost of € 14.98 crore for construction of Vit-Te-Yojna-Bhawan'*
and requested to remit हैं 3.75 crore i.e. 25 per cent of the cost of the land
within 30 days from the date of issue of the offer letter so that the allotment
letter could be issued. The remaining 75 per cent cost was to be paid within
30 days from the date of allotment without interest.

It was further noticed that instead of depositing हैं 3.75 crore, the Special
Secretary-cum-Director (Treasury and Accounts), Punjab (Director) deposited
the entire tentative cost of = 14.98 crore with the Estate Officer on
9 March 2007. However, EO could not allot the land in question, as there
were 200 colony huts on the allotted site. EO allotted (10 December 2009) an
alternate site measuring 1.737 acre of land valuing हैं 10.09 crore in
Sector 33-A and after adjusting the value of the allotted land balance amount
of = 4.89 crore was refunded to the Director on 27 May 2010. The action of
the Director resulted in blockade of funds’? and avoidable burden of interest of
हूँ 2.72 crore (calculated on average borrowing rates of the State Government).

On this being pointed out (March 2014), the Director, Punjab State Planning
Board stated that efforts were being made to recover the interest from
Chandigarh Administration. The reply was not acceptable as Chandigarh

of State funds and avoidable interest of € 2.72 crore paid by the State on its

3.5 Implementation of Food Safety and Standards Act, 2006

In order to consolidate the laws" relating to food and to establish Food Safety
and Standards Authority of India (FSSAI) for laying down science based
standards for articles of food and to regulate their manufacture, storage,
distribution, sale and import and to ensure availability of safe and wholesome
food for human consumption, Government of India (GOI) enacted
(August 2006) Food Safety and Standards (FSS) Act, 2006 (Act) and framed
(May 2011) Rules and Regulations thereunder effective from 5 August 2011
to the whole of India.

With a view to assess the efficiency and effectiveness in the implementation of
the Act, an audit was conducted (November 2013-May 2014) by test-checking
the records of the Commissioner, Food Safety (Commissioner), State Food
Laboratory (SFL) and Designated Officers (DO) in six (out of 22) districts,
selected by adopting Stratified Random Sampling without Replacement
method, covering the period 2011-12 (5 August 2011) to 2013-14.

3.5.1 Financial Management
3.5.1.1 Lapsed budget provisions

The State Government after deciding (February 2012) to set up a Food and
Drug Authority in the State, made budget provisions of five crore each year
during 2012-13 and 2013-14. It was, however, observed that the Nodal
Officer, Food Safety requested (January 2013) the Principal Secretary Health
(PSH) to release funds of % one crore from the budget provisions of
% five crore. However, the Finance Department (FD) declining the request of

‘4 The Prevention of Food Adulteration Act (PFA), 1954; PFA Rules, 1955; and various

were repealed with effect from 5 August 2011.

Department. But no proposal for establishing the Food Safety Department
was submitted to the Government/FD (May 2014). As a result, budget
provisions of % five crore during the years 2012-13 and 2013-14 lapsed.

3.5.2 Licensing and registration

3.5.2.1 Non-conducting of survey

Section 31 provides that no person shall commence or carry on any food
business except under a license. Section 30 (2) (b) of the Act provides that the
Commissioner of Food Safety shall carry out survey of the industrial units
engaged in the manufacture or processing of food in the State to find out
compliance by such units of the standards notified by the Food Authority for
various articles of food.

82


Chapter-3 : Compliance Audit

Audit of records of the Commissioner showed that neither any survey was
conducted (January 2014) nor any database with regard to total number of
food business operators (FBO) in the State was available with the
Commissioner. Though 10861 licenses and 85066 registrations were reported
(March 2014) to have been granted up to 23 January 2014, but in the absence
of the said database, FBOs running food business without licenses/
registration'® could not be ruled out.

In reply, the Department stated (March 2014) that all DOs of the State were
being directed to conduct such survey in the field.

3.5.2.2 Irregular issue of licenses

Regulation 2.1.2 (3) of the FSS (Licensing and Registration of Food
Businesses) Regulations, 2011 provides that license for commencing or
carrying on food business, which falls under Schedule 1, shall be granted by
the Central Licensing Authority (FSSAI).

Audit of records showed that in three districts (out of six test-checked
districts), 60'7 licenses were granted by DOs to FBOs for the business falling
under the purview of FSSAI during 2012-14 in contravention of the provisions
ibid. Moreover, compliance by the concerned DOs to the instructions
(July 2012) of GOI to cancel such licences was awaited (May 2014).

DO Anrritsar stated (May 2014) that the licenses were issued inadvertently,
while Bathinda and Patiala noted (April 2014) the point for compliance.

3.5.3 Laboratory and sample analysis
3.5.3.1 Short achievement of targets for collection of samples

As per the provisions under Section 38 of the Act, the Department fixed the
monthly target for collection of 1160 samples'* of any article of food or
substance by Food Safety Officer (FSO) in the State of Punjab for analysing
the same from the Food Analyst (FA).

Audit of records of the Commissioner showed that the Department was not
maintaining any data in respect of samples collected and analysed in the State
of Punjab. However, from the information obtained from FA, it was noticed
that the shortfall in achievement of targets remained up to 80 per cent at State
level and up to 93 per cent in six selected districts during the period 2011-12
(8/2011) to 2013-14. Out of 6952 samples collected in six selected districts,
900 samples were found adulterated (substandard: 726 and unsafe: 174). Of
these, against 576 cases (substandard: 432 and unsafe: 144) where prosecution
was launched, 234 cases (substandard: 234 and unsafe: Nil) had been decided.
In remaining 324 cases, action taken or reasons for not launching prosecution
were called for; the reply was awaited (December 2014).

License is issued to all food business operators except petty food business operators, who
are only required to get themselves registered with the authority.
(i)Amrritsar: 26 (2013-14); (ii)Bathinda: 19 (2012-14); and (iii) Patiala: 15 (2013-14).
The targets fixed (May 2010) for 20 districts under The Prevention of Food Adulteration
Act, 1954 being followed by the Department, were revised (April 2014) to 1160 samples
for 22 districts under the FSS Act, 2006.

83


Audit Report-Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2014

The Commissioner while admitting the facts stated (May 2014) that the targets
would be achieved as additional FSOs had been deployed.

3.5.3.2 Delayed analysis of samples from referral laboratory

Rule 2.4.5 (3) provides that FA-in-charge of the accredited laboratory shall
analyse the sample within 14 days from the date of the receipt of the sample
and in case of delay, the in-charge of laboratory shall inform the DO and the
Commissioner giving reasons and specify the time to be taken for analysis.

Audit of records of six test-checked districts showed that:

> In Bathinda and Jalandhar districts, analysis reports in respect of five '°
(out of seven) adulterated samples sent (February-December 2013) to referral
laboratory (Ghaziabad) were neither received nor were the reasons for delay
intimated by the referral laboratory even after more than 4-14 months
(April 2014).

> In Ludhiana district, analysis reports in respect of 12 (out of 20)
adulterated samples sent (September 2013-January 2014) to referral laboratory
(Ghaziabad) were received as “unsafe” with a delay ranging between 39 and
115 days. These cases were under process for launching prosecution against
FBOs (April 2014).

In reply, DO Bathinda stated (April 2014) that no action could be taken
against FBOs before receipt of reports. DOs Jalandhar and Ludhiana stated
(April 2014) that the matter would be taken up with the higher
authorities/concerned laboratory. The replies of DOs were not conclusive as
they did not even pursue the matter with the referral laboratory for timely
analysis. Thus, due to late analysis of samples, FBOs might have continued to
sell their stock during this time gap and sale of adulterated food could not be
tuled out.

3.5.4 Prosecution

3.5.4.1 Prosecution against offenders of adulterated samples

Sections 42 (3) and 59 of the Act provides that DO, after scrutiny of the report
of FA, shall decide as to whether the contravention is punishable with
imprisonment or fine only; and in the case of contravention punishable with
imprisonment, he shall send his recommendations within fourteen days to the
Commissioner for sanctioning prosecution.

(0) Audit of records of six test-checked districts showed that 51 samples in
Amritsar district (October 2011-September 2012) and two samples in Patiala
district (November 2011) were sent to FA, Punjab for analysis. But the results
of analysis were not found recorded (Amritsar) and erased (Patiala) in the
sample register. When enquired, DO Amritsar stated (May 2014) that the
reports had not been received from FA, whereas DO Patiala stated that
explanation would be sought from the dealing assistants.

19

Bathinda: 2 (Unsafe); and Jalandhar: 3 (2 Unsafe and 1 Sub-standard).
